The π-type Rydberg series has no evidence of this valence π^* contribution in the I[90] ion-yield (Π, ΔΛ=+1) spectrum. On the other hand, the σ-type Rydberg series is obscured by strong σ^* resonance features, B and C, in the I[0] ion-yield (Σ, ΔΛ=0) spectrum. We must take account of the Rydberg–valence mixing or avoided curve crossing between the same symmetry states, as shown in [Fig. 15](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0015).

[Figure 14.](#chapter02.html__back10) High-resolution ARPIS of O[2] in the ![img](images/c02/inline700.gif) and Rydberg excitation region.

![img](images/c02/nfg014.gif)

[Figure 15.](#chapter02.html__back015) Schematic potential energy curves for the O 1s→ π^*, σ^*, and Rydberg excited states in O[2] [26]. Interaction between σ^* and 3pσ Rydberg states is strong in the quartet (Q) manifold.

![img](images/c02/nfg015.gif)

[Figure 16.](#chapter02.html__back016) Comparison between theoretical and experimental I[0](ΔΛ=0, Σ) spectra of O 1s→σ^* and Rydberg region of O[2] [108]. The theoretical spectrum is decomposed into the quartet (Q) and doublet (D) ionization channels.

![img](images/c02/nfg016.gif)

[Figure 17.](#chapter02.html__back017) Potential energy scheme of the unusual spectral profile in the lower O 1s→ σ^* region [108].

![img](images/c02/nfg017.gif)

[Figure 18.](#chapter02.html__backb018) The S 2p ARPIS of the S 2p ![img](images/c02/inline817.gif) excitation region of SO[2] [84]. Molecular field, EX and SO splittings based on ab initio Breit–Pauli calculations with correlation corrections are shown. Singlet components shown in percent are compared with the experiment.

![img](images/c02/nfg018.gif)

[Figure 19.](#chapter02.html__back019) Angular distributions of the unresolved X and A states for Σ←Σ (left) and Π←Σ (right) ionization channels, in comparison to those of the B state, measured at the photon energy of 305 eV.

![img](images/c02/nfg019.gif)

In the I[90] (Π) spectrum, several Rydberg peaks converging to the ⁴Σ^− (D) and ²Σ^− (D) core-ionized states, Rydberg (Q) and Rydberg (D), are resolved. Considering that the exchange splitting in the Rydberg series is nearly the same as in the core-ionized states of ⁴Σ^− and ²Σ^− (1.1 eV) and the intensity ratio of the Rydberg (Q) to Rydberg (D) states is almost 2:1, peaks observed in the I[90] spectrum are assigned mainly to npπ (Q) and n'pπ (D) Rydberg states. In the following discussion on the Rydberg–valence mixing in the σ channel, we start from the diabatic representation, such as σ^* (D), σ^* (Q), 3s(D), 3s(Q), 3p(D), and 3p(Q), where σ^*, 3s, and 3p are 2pσ^* valence, 3sσ Ry, and 3pσ Rydberg electrons.

1\. Lower σ^* Region

The symmetry-resolved ion-yield spectrum I[0] in [Fig. 14](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0014) clearly exhibits that the two main features B and C have σ symmetry. These intensities should come from the valence σ^* component, irrespective of complicated Rydberg-valence mixing. We could simply assign feature B to σ^* (D) and feature C to σ^* (Q), considering that the transition probability of 1s→σ^* (Q) is twice as large as that of 1s→σ^* (D). One of the most puzzling spectral characteristics in the energy region in question is the lower-energy part ![img](images/sim.gif)539 eV observed in the experiment: a very long energy tail and a sharp first peak followed by some smaller peaks [23, 26, 33, 34, 91, 102, 104–116]. The accepted interpretation of this feature is that the main intensity arises from the dissociative σ^* (D) resonance, which is lower than σ^* (Q) due to exchange interaction of the σ^* electron [23, 83]. On the other hand, the fine features ![img](images/sim.gif)539 eV were interpreted as a weak vibrational progression in the bound 3s(Q) state embedded in the σ^* (D) manifold [23] and a Fano profile due to weak interaction between the bound and continuum-like state [26]. The interaction between the 3s(Q) and σ^* (D) states could be small, because they have different ion cores Q and D and 3s and 2pσ^* have different orbital characters [26]. However, σ^* (D) can be mixed with σ^* (Q) [83, 102, 108], and the small σ^* (Q) component would contribute to interaction with 3s(Q). On the other hand, the strong Rydberg–valence mixing between 2pσ^* (Q) and 3pσ (Q) is found in the higher energy part ![img](images/sim.gif)542 eV [26, 34, 91, 105], as shown in [Fig. 15](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0015).

The theoretical spectra produced by the coupled wave-packet propagation in the diabatic states [108] are shown in [Fig. 16](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0016) in comparison with the experimental Σ spectra. In [Fig. 16](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0016), the partial contributions of the Q and D channels are shown by ignoring the weak coupling between them. The overall spectral shapes of both partial cross-sections have roughly Gaussian shapes owing to absorption to the dissociative states σ^* (Q) and σ^* (D). The Gaussian shapes are split and the intensity is reduced near the crossing points between the dissociative σ^* states and the bound Rydberg states, because the latter transition dipoles are smaller compared to the former state. Note that the structures below the potential crossing point exhibit a smooth continuum (dissociative) shape, while the structures above the crossing point have a vibrational character shaped by the interfering coupled wave packets simultaneously evolving in the core-excited states. The largest splits in the middle of the spectral packets of both Q and D channels near 540 eV are caused by mixing with the 3p(Q) and 3p(D) Rydberg states, respectively, whose interactions with the dissociative states are largest ([Fig. 17](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0017)). The low-energy tail <539 eV of the total spectrum has a continuum profile resulting from both dissociative σ^* (D) and σ^* (Q) states. We would like to pay attention to the spectral profile of the D channel where the vibrational structure is almost invisible ([Fig. 16](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0016)). It is a weak vibrational progression on the top of the D spectrum in the regions 539–540 and 541–543 eV.

The formation of the first resonance-like feature in the theoretical spectrum can be better understood with the help of [Fig. 17](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0017) [108]. When the X-ray energy increases from the region below the crossing S, the overlap between the ground-state wave function and the continuum wave functions grows, because the classical turning point in the dissociative state moves to shorter bond lengths, closer to the equilibrium geometry. Near the crossing point S, the transition dipole abruptly falls, due to the increased 3s(Q) component, and causes a dip in the absorption spectrum. Thus, the initial increase of the cross-section is followed by a sudden decrease, after which the cross-section grows again. The broad tail to the lower energy is mostly due to the σ^* (D) state of dissociative character. The intensity for the excitation to the dissociative state or repulsive state σ^* (Q) is now reduced ![img](images/sim.gif)540 eV due to the intensity transfer from the σ^* (Q) to the 3p(Q) state near the crossing point S1 ([Fig. 17](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0017)). When tuning the photon energy at feature B, the 1s→σ^* (D) state is possibly dissociated to the atomic O 1s→2p ³P excited state with no curve crossing, as shown in [Fig. 15](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0015), resulting in atomic Auger decay [103, 114, 115].

2\. Higher σ^* Region

In contrast to the mixing related to the D channel, the higher and stronger 1s→σ^* (Q) state can interact with several Rydberg states with the same ion core Q, especially, npσ (Q) series with the same orbital character. In the I[0] spectrum, the npσ (Q) Rydberg and n'pσ (D) series show single peaks similarly to the npπ (Q) and n'pπ (D) Rydberg series, except that the 3pσ (Q) Rydberg transition cannot be assigned to a single peak. The potential energy curves shown in [Fig. 15](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0015) [26] indicate that the 1s→σ^* (Q) repulsive state can strongly interact with the 1s→3pσ (Q) Rydberg state in the FC region. Then, a bound state (upper state) is produced through the avoided curve crossing between σ^* (Q) and 3pσ (Q) and several vibrational excitations become allowed. Thus, the 3pσ (Q) Rydberg state is strongly perturbed by the repulsive σ^* (Q) state with the same ion core, and has an unusual vibrational enhancement as clearly observed in the I[0] spectrum in [Fig. 14](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0014).

A similar Rydberg–valence mixing is more or less expected in other npσ Rydberg states; in fact, the npσ (Q) Rydberg series is much stronger than the n'pσ (D) Rydberg series. Due to strong Rydberg–valence mixings through the avoided curve crossings in the Q channel, the repulsive 1s→σ^* (Q) excited state cannot be isolated from the Rydberg states. The 1s→σ^* (Q) excitation disperses its intensity mainly to the 1s→npσ (Q) Rydberg series, which has bound-state character, and reduces dissociative character. Therefore, the molecular Auger decay is observed when tuning the photon energy at feature C.

VI. ARPIS of SO[2]

A. Core–Valence Exchange versus Spin–Orbit Interactions

The spin–orbit (SO) splitting of the core electrons is directly observed in XPS. Even in the 2p photoabsorption spectra, the 2p SO splitting of third-row and heavier elements is large and is easily distinguishable. The singlet and triplet 2p excited states are strongly and indistinguishably mixed with each other through SO or jj coupling. However, in the second-row elements, such as phosphorus and sulfur, the 2p SO is not satisfactorily analyzed due to a comparable intraatomic core–valence EX splitting. Spin–Orbit is still a major factor in the core-to-Rydberg excited state with a small ST (EX) splitting, but is only one of some important factors in the core-to-valence excited state with a relatively large ST (EX) splitting [116–120], as also discussed in O[2] in comparison with S[2] and Se[2]. In recent high-resolution photoelectron spectroscopy, it is possible to reveal another small splitting in the 2p[3/2] manifold due to the molecular field (MF) effect [121–124].

The 2p excitation spectra of molecules containing second-row elements, such as sulfur and phosphorus, are thought to be generally characterized by the presence of two closely related manifolds of the SO pair, 2p[3/2] (lower in energy, twice as strong) and 2p[1/2]. The SO interaction is inherently of atomic core character. Spectral assignments in the 2p excitation spectra are often based on identification of corresponding lines in the 2p[3/2] and 2p[1/2] manifolds, which are clearly resolved in recent high-resolution 2p photoelectron spectra, together with a very small splitting in the 2p[3/2] manifold due to the MF splitting [121–124]. However, in some excited states of the P 2p and S 2p excitation spectra of PF[3], SF[6], OPF[3], SPF[3], NSF[3] and other related systems, it is found to be too difficult to assign the 2p[3/2] and 2p[1/2] SO pair [116–120]. In the 2p excitation, the excited electron contributes to the core–valence EX and is coupled with a singlet or triplet spin in excitation from the closed-shell ground state, whereas in the 2p ionization there is not a corresponding EX interaction. When the ST (EX) splitting (Δ[ST]) is much larger than the SO splitting, the ST mixing through SO is negligible and no SO pair is observed, as precisely discussed for P 2p excitation of PF[3] [118]. The 2p excitation spectra of third-row and heavier elements are much simpler, because the deeper core increases the SO interaction and weakens the core–valence EX interaction. In this sense, both EX and SO are exclusively important in the 2p excitation of the second-row elements by chance.

Exchange involving the 2p core electron is anisotropic, depending on the relation between the 2p orbital direction and molecular structure. In some sulfur containing molecules, it is experimentally revealed that the S 2p and excited electrons have EX compatible to the 2p SO interaction for parallel 2pπ→π^* transitions and EX smaller than SO for perpendicular 2pσ→π^* transitions. In other words, the jj coupling scheme is not applicable to the parallel transitions. Thus, we can expect the following general scheme for anisotropic EX interaction versus 2p SO interaction:

1. Perpendicular transition (I[90]): EX < SO

The jj coupling scheme is valid.

Singlet (bright) components distributed equally to lower and higher states.

2. Parallel transition (I[0]): EX > SO

The jj coupling scheme is broken (intermediate coupling).

Excitation energy is blue shifted due to large EX.

Lower states have more triplet (dark) components.

Higher states have more singlet (bright) components.

B. jj-Coupling versus Intermediate Coupling

The S 1s ARPIS spectra of a C![img](images/c02/inline819.gif) symmetry planar molecule, SO[2], have shown three valence states below the Rydberg manifold, 3b[1] (π^*), 9a[1], and 4b[2] [125]. [Figure 18](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0018) shows S 2p ARPIS of the S 2p→3b[1] (π^*) region [84]. Although high-resolution photoabsorption spectra in this region have been analyzed [126] by referring to an already-reported theoretical analysis [127] based on nonrelativistic HF solutions combined with an empirical SO splitting parameter, detailed SO and EX characteristics have not yet been discussed. The S 2p →3b[1]π^* excited states have the ^(1,3)B[1], ^(1,3)A[2], and ^(1,3)A[1] symmetries using the 2p orbitals of a[1], b[2], and b[1] symmetries. From the X¹ A[1] ground state the singlet ¹A[2] excited state is electric dipole forbidden. The ¹A[1]←X¹A[1] and ¹B[1]←X¹A[1] excitations are dipole allowed, being transitions parallel and perpendicular to the polarization vector of photons, respectively. Even after taking into account the SO interaction, the singlet ¹B[1], ¹A[2], and ¹A[1] excited states are not mixed with one another, but each singlet state is mixed with two dipole-forbidden triplet states of ³B[1], ³A[2], and ³A[1] through SO, resulting in three electronic states for each singlet symmetry.

In [Fig. 18](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0018), the MF, EX, and SO interactions are taken into account step by step in comparison with the S 2p ARPIS of SO[2]. First, the small MF splitting in the 2p ionization is shown without either SO or EX. Second, considering the excited electron in 3b[1]π^* explicitly, the EX(+MF) splitting at the zero SO limit is shown. As expected, the ST (EX) splitting in the parallel 2pb[1]→3b[1]π^*(^(1,3)A[1]) transition is much larger than ST in the perpendicular transitions, 2pa[1]→3b[1]π^*(^(1,3)B[1]) and 2pb[2]→3b[1]π^*(^(1,3)A[2]). In [Fig. 18](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0018), the SO interaction is finally taken into account in addition to MF and EX [124]. The ¹A[1] manifold indicates a rather large ST splitting ![img](images/sim.gif)0.9 eV; therefore, the lowest of the three SO states in the parallel excitation hardly borrows intensity from the dipole-allowed ¹A[1] component and is almost invisible. In the ¹A[1] manifold observed in the parallel polarization I[0], the peak intensity in the 2p[1/2] region is comparable to (a little larger than) that in the 2p[3/2] region, in good agreement with the theoretical prediction. On the other hand, in the ¹B[1] manifold observed in the perpendicular polarization I[90], the ST (EX) splitting is small, ![img](images/sim.gif)0.1 eV, and SO is the most significant. Then, the jj coupling scheme predicts that the 2p[3/2] (L) region is much stronger than the 2p[1/2] (H) region, though the intensity ratio I(H)/I(L) is not 0.5 (at the zero EX limit), but ![img](images/sim.gif)0.65\. In this case, even the lowest state in the 2p[3/2] region can get intensity from the ¹B[1] component and is observable.

VII. Some Extensions Related to ARPIS Technique

The angular anisotropy in the emission direction of photoproducts formed following inner-shell excitation and ionization can be applied to various kinds of investigation in molecular inner-shell photoprocesses. Since Siegel and co-workers [128] reported on their hypothetical experiment for the angular distributions of photoelectrons from fixed-in-space molecules in 1976, experimentalists started to think of how to realize this situation. In 1992, Zähringer et al. [129] performed theoretical calculations for angle-resolved Auger rates from spatially oriented molecules, where the authors suggested that the full angular distribution of Auger electrons is measurable by coincidence detection between the momenta of the Auger electron and atomic ion that is produced by the prompt dissociation following the molecular Auger decay. The first experimental realization of this kind was reported as the angular distribution measurements of photoelectrons from fixed-in-space molecules, which was achieved by introducing the coincidence detection between the photoelectron and fragment ion [130]. After this pioneering work, many investigations for the molecular frame photoelectron angular distribution (MFPAD), which are essentially based on the same concept as [130], have been carried out intensively [131–157]. Since the applications of the angular anisotropy in the dissociation process following the inner-shell photoexcitation, some recent investigations other than MFPAD are summarized in the following Section VII.

A. Auger Electron Emission from Spatially Fixed Molecules

It is quite natural to extend the experimental technique for measuring MFPAD to the Auger electron emission. The most detailed information on the MOs involved in the process is obtainable by measuring the angular distributions of Auger electrons from fixed-in-space molecules. As demonstrated in this chapter, thanks to the rapid progress of the experimental techniques associated with synchrotron radiation, vibrational spectroscopy in the inner-shell excitation region of low-Z molecules has become feasible. The findings through the studies on the resonant Auger processes and their theoretical analyses impel us to give up the traditional idea that the molecular dissociation begins with the Auger final state when the Auger transition is terminated [158]. This means that the Auger decay cannot be treated separately from the primary photoexcitation process. However, it is widely believed that the decay process following inner-shell ionization, namely, the normal Auger decay, can be interpreted as a two-step process in which the Auger decay is treated independently of the initial ionization process. The experiment to show the limitation of this picture has been performed on CO after C1s ionization [159]. The de-excitation of the C (1s)^(−1) core–hole state predominantly populates the three lowest singlet states X¹Σ(5σ^(−2)), A¹Π(5σ^(−1)1π^(−1)), B¹Σ(5σ^(−1)4σ^(−1)) of the doubly ionized CO molecule.

The angular distributions after both σ and π ionization for the unresolved X and A states are shown in [Fig. 19](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0019), in comparison to those for the B state: the angular distribution patterns for the X and A states are almost isotropic, but those for the B state are very rich in structure and remarkably different from each other. The latter observation completely differs from both regular Auger electron angular distributions from randomly oriented molecules, and the theoretical prediction for the fixed-in-space angular distributions of Auger electrons by Kuznetsov and Cherepkov [160], where the angular distribution patterns after both σ and π ionization should be the same. Sundin et al. [161] have demonstrated with vibrational resolution that the PCI effect distorts the line shapes of both the photoelectron and Auger decay spectra recorded at the C1s shape resonance position. The results for the B state in [Fig. 19](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0019) may imply that the PCI effect affects the Auger electron not only in its energy distribution, but also in its angular distribution. In order to examine such new findings in more details, Guillemin et al. [159] performed the similar experiments at various photon energies. It was found that the angular patterns for the X and A states exhibit no photon energy dependences, while there is a dramatic photon energy dependence of the angular distribution patterns for the B state in each ionization channel. They concluded that their observation can be a direct evidence for a dynamical angular correlation between the Auger electron, the residual ion, and the photoelectron. The authors believed these results to be evidence for the breakdown of the two-step model for the molecular Auger decay and merit further theoretical investigation.

Weber et al. [162] later performed a similar experiment using a different experimental setup based on the imaging detection, named COLTRIMS (Cold Target Recoil Ion Momentum Spectroscopy [163]), which led to controversial results for the dependence of the angular distributions of Auger electrons on the photoionization channels and the photon energy. Since then, several groups have studied the Auger electron angular distributions in the molecular frame for different systems [164–170]. In order to gain complete information on the photoionization and Auger decay processes, a new experimental setup toward an extended analysis of the Auger-electron–photoelectron angular correlation and emission of Auger electrons and photoelectrons from fixed-in-space molecules seems to be ready to operate [171]. The controversy in the angular distributions for the Auger electrons following the C 1s photoionization of fixed-in-space CO molecules [159, 162] will be solved in the near future.

B. Metastable Fragment Detection

As demonstrated in Section III–VI, definite assignments for the discrete resonances have been given by the combined utilization of the high-resolution ARPIS method with sophisticated theoretical calculations. The performance of the ARPIS method is, however, limited by the inherent background owing to the 1s ionization continuum, which tends to hide weaker multiexcitation structures. In particular, the large cross-section of the shape resonance severely prevents one from identifying multiexcitation structures associated with the parallel transition. The background contribution from the 1s ionization continuum can be reduced by measuring partial electron yields. Neeb et al. [65] gained the partial electron yield curve by monitoring 384-eV Auger electrons from N[2], revealing similar double-excitation structures as observed in the angle-resolved ion detection measurement, but with a smaller background [67]. However, the poor resolution in their study hindered the clear identification of the doubly excited states. Although it was considered that the 384-eV electrons were due to atomic Auger decay following ultrafast dissociation, Naves de Brito et al. [68] concluded later that the corresponding electrons are associated with the formation of the C![img](images/c02/inline874.gif) and 2²Π[g] states of N![img](images/c02/inline876.gif).

The multielectron processes due to the electron correlation, such as the shake-up and shake-off transitions, are known to happen not only in the primary inner-shell hole creation processes, but also in their relaxation processes. In the case of the shake-off transition, where two electrons are ejected simultaneously, the great intensity of the peaks in the zero-kinetic-energy (ZEKE) electron spectrum arises through energy sharing between the two emitted electrons as their kinetic energy distribution is peaked with one electron having a low kinetic energy electron and the other having a correspondingly high kinetic energy. Thus, the ZEKE spectroscopy is very sensitive to this two-electron process. Additional information on the multiply excited states in the core-ionization continua can be gained by the combination of ARPIS with ZEKE, which can be called “symmetry-resolved zero-kinetic-energy (SR–ZEKE) photoelectron spectroscopy. This spectroscopy is realized by the coincident detection between the ZEKE photoelectrons and angle-resolved fragment ions. Although the symmetries of the multiply excited states and the photoelectron satellites in the K-shell ionization regions of CO [172] and N[2] [173] was investigated under a high-resolution condition, clear symmetry identifications for the multielectron processes in the continuum were not given owing to the poor statistics of the SR–ZEKE spectra obtained.

Here, it is demonstrated that angle-resolved detection of metastable fragments is an effective spectroscopic tool for probing multiply excited states in molecules. It has already been shown that the production of metastable fragments N^* is favored after the decay of the core-excited N[2] states lying below the 1s ionization threshold [174]. The N^* fragments are produced through the resonant Auger decay of the core-excited states into N![img](images/c02/inline881.gif) states that subsequently undergo dissociation into N^++N^* [174]. The angle-resolved metastable yield curves above the 1s threshold exhibit multiexcitation structures with symmetry information. It is presented that the observation of metastable fragments enables the identification of multiexcitation structures with no significant background contribution from the ionization continuum [175].

The experimental setup for metastable detection is essentially the same as indicated in [Fig. 1](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0001), but the applying voltages to the grids and MCP are different. The retarding grids were held at +80 V and the front plates of the two microchannel plate stacks at −2 kV. This arrangement prevents the detection of charged particles, ensuring that the detectors are sensitive only to neutral metastable particles with sufficient internal energy (>![img](images/sim.gif)9 eV) and fluorescence photons in the vacuum ultraviolet (UV) and soft X-ray ranges. The geometry of the setup ensures that only (energetic) fragment N^* metastables are detected, since the parent molecules maintain the velocity distribution inherent to the effusive beam. [Figure 20](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0020) denotes the metastable yield curves recorded in the photon energy region of 405.7–430 eV in N[2] at 0 and 90^(![img](images/compfn.gif)) with respect to the electric vector. The photon energy resolution was set to ![img](images/sim.gif)200 meV. Below the 1s threshold, peaks due to Rydberg excitations are observed. Resonant Auger decay of the Rydberg states into N![img](images/c02/inline890.gif) states and the subsequent dissociation into N^++N^* result in the N^* formation [174]. Rydberg excitations associated with parallel transitions are observed in metastable yield at 0^(![img](images/compfn.gif)), and perpendicular transitions at 90^(![img](images/compfn.gif)), similar to the ion-yield curves of [Fig. 5](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0005). This result implies that the emission directions of the metastable fragments essentially reflect the initial molecular orientations at the moment of photoexcitation, and that metastable observation enables us to resolve the symmetries of the Rydberg states. This conservation of molecular alignment indicates that the N![img](images/c02/inline895.gif) dissociation following the Rydberg excitations is faster than the molecular rotation. Note that the finite acceptance angles of the detectors mix small amounts of parallel and perpendicular transition components into the 90^(![img](images/compfn.gif)) and 0^(![img](images/compfn.gif)) curves, respectively. In addition, a slight reduction of the initial orientations due to molecular rotation and/or an incomplete discrimination against fluorescence emissions with long lifetimes may be involved in the obtained spectra.

[Figure 20.](#chapter02.html__back11) Angle-resolved metastable yield curves of N[2] measured at 0 and 90^(![img](images/compfn.gif)) with respect to the electric vector. The photon energy resolution was set to 200 meV.

![img](images/c02/nfg020.gif)

[Figure 21.](#chapter02.html__back021) Net O^− yield curve obtained in the O 1s excitation region of CO[2], and O^− images measured at the three photon energies indicated. The photon bandwidth was set to ![img](images/sim.gif)0.16 eV, except for the image observations at hν=548.9 and 553.5 eV, where the bandwidth was set to ![img](images/sim.gif)0.5 eV.

![img](images/c02/nfg021.gif)

Higher Rydberg states produce metastable fragments more efficiently [174, 176], resulting in the large peaks around the 1s threshold on the metastable yield curves. The same N![img](images/c02/inline900.gif) states formed by resonant Auger decay from the high Rydberg states also can be produced by the recapture of slow photoelectrons due to postcollision interaction [176]; the tails at the high-energy sides of the large peaks reflect the recapture probability [174]. The metastable yields decrease above the 1s ionization threshold, in contrast to the ionization continuum behaviors of the ion yields. This is because inner-shell photoionization rarely results in the formation of N^*, and therefore the 1s ionization continuum hardly affects the metastable observation. In contrast, multiply excited states can produce N^* if the states undergo resonant Auger decay into N![img](images/c02/inline903.gif) states dissociating to N^++N^*. Many structures above the ionization threshold are observed in [Fig. 20](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0020), and are attributable to multiply excited states.

Hikosaka et al. [175] also measured the metastable yield curves in the doubly excitation region (412.5–417.5 eV) with a narrower photon bandwidth (40 meV) and better statistics. The benefit of metastable observation is more pronounced for the Σ-symmetry states: the 0^(![img](images/compfn.gif)) curve clearly reveals the double-excitation structures that are seen as a shoulder (label E) on the shape resonance enhancement of the 0^(![img](images/compfn.gif)) ion yield curve in [Fig. 5](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0005). A configuration-interaction calculation locates in this energy range one Σ-symmetry state with the electronic configuration of ![img](images/c02/inline909.gif) and two Π-symmetry states with ![img](images/c02/inline911.gif) as discussed in Section III. These states should appear as broad peaks reflecting photoexcitation to the repulsive walls of the potential energy curves. Narrow peaks are superimposed on broad structures. The single vibrational progression starting at 414.01 eV [17] is ascribable to the Rydberg-type doubly excited state ![img](images/c02/inline912.gif). Through the data analysis, it is found that two new vibrational progressions, one Σ and one Π state, are discernible at 416–417 eV on the metastable yield curves [175]. It is nicely demonstrated that the observation of metastable fragments enables us to identify multiexcitation structures almost free from background contributions from the ionization continuum.

C. Negative Ion Detection

The observation of negative fragment ions can also be a sensitive probe of the multiply excited states embedded in the inner-shell ionization continuum, in the case where the molecules decay through the emission of stable negative fragment ions [177]. Formation of negative fragment ion has been explored in the inner-shell regions of simple molecules, since the first observation in the S 2p region of SO[2] [178]. The investigations have revealed that negative ion formation is enhanced at core-excited states and not associated with inner-shell ionization. While it had been presumed that negative ion is emitted from singly charged ion states produced by resonant Auger decay of core-excited states, the direct evidence to the mechanism was recently offered by a negative–positive ion coincidence measurement [179]. Lindle and co-workers proved that the observation of negative fragment ions can be a probe to postcollision interaction [180] and to doubly excited states embedded in inner-shell ionization continua [181].

Traditionally, measurements of negative ion yields have been performed with mass spectrometric methods. Ordinary mass spectrometry, however, rarely possesses the ability to determine kinetic energies and angular distributions of negative ions, although the information derived from these species is useful for determining the electronic characters of the relevant core-excited states. It is also interesting to study the dynamical property on the negative ion productions. Recently, Hikosaka et al. [177] introduced the imaging technique into the observation of the negative ions in the molecular inner-shell region, in order to gain information on the kinetic energies and angular distributions of the particles concerned. As an example, the imaging observation on the negative ion formations in the vicinity of the O 1s ionization thresholds of CO[2] is demonstrated here.

In a previous work [182], not only O^− fragments, but also C^− fragments, have been observed in the O 1s photoexcitation region of CO[2], where the relative intensity was not noted. However, only O^− formation is discernible on the time spectra measured at several photon energies in the O 1s region; Hikosaka et al. [177] concluded that C^− formation is much weaker than O^− formation. A net O^− yield curve in the O 1s region is shown in [Fig. 21](#chapter02.html_c02-fig-0021), together with the O^− images measured at three photon energies. The spectral features observed agree with those in the previously obtained spectrum [182]. The π^* and Rydberg resonances below the O 1s ionization threshold are essentially the same as those interpreted well by photoabsorption and ion-yield measurements [183, 184]. The O^− image observed at the π^* resonance shows O^− emissions preferably into the direction perpendicular to the electric vector, as expected from the symmetry consideration. The kinetic energy distribution deduced from the image is similar to that at the C 1s → π^* resonance. The broad structures above the ionization threshold are allocated to doubly excited states, while the photoabsorption and photoionization spectra indicate only the structure around hν=547 eV [185, 186]. Weak preferences in the O^− emissions perpendicular to the electric vector can be seen on both the images, suggesting superior perpendicular transitions at these photon energies.

Chapter 3

Geometric optimal control of simple quantum systems

Dominique Sugny

Laboratorie Interdisciplinaire Carnot de Bourgogne (ICB), UMR 5209 CNRS-Université de BourgogneF-21078 Dijon Cedex, France

I. Introduction

Optimal control theory can be viewed as a generalization of the classical calculus of variations for problems with dynamical constraints. Optimal control theory was born in its modern version with the Pontryagin Maximum Principle (PMP) in the late 1950s [1]. Its development was originally inspired by problems of space dynamics, but it is now a key tool to study a large spectrum of applications, such as robotics, economics, and quantum mechanics. Solving an optimal control problem means finding a particular control law (i.e., a particular pulse sequence), the optimal control, such that the corresponding trajectory satisfies given boundary conditions and minimizes a cost criterion. The cost functionals of physical interests are the energy of the field and the duration of the control. The strategy for solving an optimal control problem consists in finding extremal trajectories that are solutions of a generalized Hamiltonian system subject to the maximization condition of the PMP. In a second step, one selects among the extremals, the ones that effectively minimize the cost criterion. Although its implementation looks straightforward, the practical use of the PMP is far from being trivial and each control has to be analyzed using geometric and numerical methods. The first applications of optimal control theory in the control of quantum dynamics began in the mid-1980's. Continuous advances have been made both theoretically and experimentally [2–4]. The optimal equations can be solved by purely numerical techniques, such as the gradient algorithm, the monotonically convergent algorithms [5–7], or by using geometric tools [1, 8–12]. Due to the complexity of realistic systems with multiple degrees of freedom, the numerical approach 129 seems to be the only possible way to achieve control. More geometric aspects of the control can be formulated only for the simplest quantum systems having few levels (typically two or three) or consisting of coupled spin 1/2 particles [13]. The description of the geometric aspects of the control problems has not been widely used in the chemical physics literature and only few results exist in quantum mechanics mainly for closed quantum systems (see [14–19] to cite a few). The aim of this chapter is to present some of the tools of geometric optimal control theory that can be used in quantum control. For that purpose, we consider some simple examples as the control by laser fields of three-level conservative systems and of two-level dissipative systems. In a first approach, we skip most of the mathematical details. The reader is referred to standard mathematical textbooks for a complete and rigorous development of geometric optimal control theory [1, 8–10, 20]. Finally, we point out that the controllability analysis that is the first part of the study of an optimal control problem will not be discussed in this chapter (see, e.g., [21–26]). The controllability problem consists in determining if there exists a path going from the initial to the target state. This property will be assumed throughout this chapter.

This chapter is organized as follows: In Section II, we introduce the PMP and we state the theorem in the different cases that can be encountered in practice. The techniques associated to the use of the PMP, that is, indirect and continuation methods or the second-order optimality conditions are also detailed. Such tools allow us to compute the extremal solutions of the PMP and to determine their local optimality. Sections III and IV are devoted to the application of the PMP to the control of quantum systems. Section III deals with the optimal control of a three-level quantum system by laser fields plus von Neumann measurements. This three-level system corresponds to the Grushin model on the sphere that is one of the basic models in geometric optimal control. In particular, global optimality results can be obtained for this model. In Section IV, we solve the problem of the time-minimum control of two-level dissipative quantum systems by one or two control fields. For this example, we use the continuation techniques and the second-order optimality conditions. A realistic example in nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) of the control of a spin 1/2 particle in a dissipative environment is also treated. Conclusion and prospective views are given in Section V.

II. The Pontryagin Maximum Principle

A. The End-Point Mapping

We consider a controlled dynamical system governed by the differential equations of the form

Definition 2 The parameter u is said to be regular on [0, T] if the image of E' is ![img](images/c03/inline055.gif) and singular otherwise.

Geometric Interpretation

An equivalent statement of the fact that the image of the map E' is ![img](images/c03/inline055.gif) is the following. A regular control means that, by varying u by a small amount δu, one can reach any point in a neighborhood of the final point x(T, x[0], u). This notion can be more precisely defined as follows. We introduce the set A of accessible states in time T from x[0]:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0010.gif)

Let x[1] = x(T) be a point reached in time T from x[0] by a regular trajectory. Then the accessibility set A(x[0], T) is a neighborhood of the point x[1], which implies that the system is locally controllable around x[1]. If x(T)![img](images/epsiv1.gif)∂A(x[0], T) (i.e., the boundary of A), then u is said to be singular on [0, T]. This case is schematically illustrated in [Fig. 2](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0002) for a regular and singular control field. The most interesting case is therefore the singular one and this case can be viewed as a first step toward the maximum principle. Indeed, a key observation for this principle is that if the trajectory (x, u) is optimal, then the point x(T, x[0], u) must belong to the boundary of the accessibility set. This assertion can be understood in the time-minimal case, where the goal is to reach the target state x[1] in minimum time. It is clear that if x[1] = x(x[0], T, u) belongs to the interior of A(x[0], T), then one can find a smaller time T'<T and a point x(x[0], T', u') of ∂A(x[0], T), such that x[1] = x(x[0], T', u') (one can draw the accessibility set at different times to be convinced of this point). This result finally implies that u is not optimal for this control problem.

[Figure 2.](#chapter03.html__back33) Schematic representation of the accessibility set A(x[0], T) in gray. The control fields u[1] and u[2] are respectively singular, and regular.

![img](images/c03/nfg002.gif)

B. The Pontryagin Maximum Principle

1\. The General Formulation

We consider the following optimal control problem of the dynamical system governed by the differential equation ![img](images/c03/inline153.gif). The goal of the problem is to determine the control law such that the state of the system goes from x(0)![img](images/epsiv1.gif)M[0] to x(T)![img](images/epsiv1.gif)M[1] in time T, which can be free or fixed, together minimizing the cost functional ![img](images/c03/inline157.gif), where F⁰ is a smooth function. Both M[0] and M[1] are two submanifolds of ![img](images/c03/inline161.gif), which can consist of a single point. Different usual examples can be given. If F⁰ = 1 with T free, then the optimal control problem consists in minimizing the control duration. An upper bound is added to the norm of the control in order to obtain finite values for the control law and nonzero control duration. If ![img](images/c03/inline164.gif), then the cost penalizes the energy of the field. If ![img](images/c03/inline165.gif) with ![img](images/c03/inline166.gif) and m = n, then we recover the standard calculus of variations. A possible function L is given by ![img](images/c03/inline169.gif).

The idea of Pontryagin is to introduce the extended system

(3)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0022.gif)

where x⁰ is the cost variable with x⁰(0) = 0\. The extended state (x, x⁰) is denoted ![img](images/c03/inline173.gif) and the extended vector field ![img](images/c03/inline174.gif). The optimal control problem consists now in minimizing the cost ![img](images/c03/inline175.gif). As mentioned above, we use the fact that if (x, u) is optimal, then the extremity ![img](images/c03/inline177.gif) of the extended trajectory belongs to the boundary of the accessibility set of the extended system. This point is illustrated in [Fig. 5](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0005) where one sees that the minimum of the cost variable x⁰ is reached for a point of the boundary. The maximum principle is a necessary condition for this property. We simply state the corresponding theorem without any proof. The proof of the PMP in the general case is a very deep and difficult mathematical result.

The function ![img](images/c03/inline196.gif) is usually called the pseudo-Hamiltonian in the literature, while the name Hamiltonian (or normal Hamiltonian) is given to the function ![img](images/c03/inline197.gif), which is obtained by replacing ![img](images/c03/inline198.gif) by its expression.

We introduce the notion of extremals:

Definition 3 An extremal of the optimal control problem is a quadruplet (x, p, p⁰, u) solution of [Eqs. (4)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0023) and [(5)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0024). If p⁰ = 0, then the extremal is said to be abnormal, while the extremal is normal if p⁰≠0.

Note that the couple (p, p⁰) is defined up to a scalar positive factor. This finding means that if the triplet (x, p, p⁰) represents an extremal, then the triplet (x, αp, αp⁰) corresponds to the same extremal. In the normal case, one usually normalizes p⁰ to −1/2 or −1 by a suitable choice of the constant α.

We also define another set of trajectories that have a very special feature.

Definition 4 A singular trajectory is a solution of the following equations:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0025.gif)

where ![img](images/c03/inline211.gif) and we add the constraint that the admissible values of u belongs to the interior of the domain U.

Note that singular trajectories do not depend on the cost of the optimal control problem. Such trajectories correspond, by definition, to singularities of the endpoint mapping introduced in Section II. A. Singular trajectories that are also extremals of the control problem are called singular extremals. Such extremals depend on the cost. Some examples of singular extremals will be given in Section IV. For example, one sees that for a system of the form ![img](images/c03/inline214.gif) in the time-minimal case, singular trajectories satisfy p·F[1](x) = 0, while singular extremals satisfy in addition p·F[0](x)+p⁰ = 0, where p⁰ is constant.

In this section, we have considered the Lagrange problem where the cost is of the form ![img](images/c03/inline218.gif). Some modifications of the theorem have to be done in the Mayer–Lagrange case, where the cost can be written as:

In particular, the final condition on the adjoint vector reads in this case

The reader is referred to standard textbooks of optimal control theory for the applications of the PMP in this case [8, 11].

Now, we particularize the PMP to the time-minimal control problem.

Proposition 1 Consider the time-minimal control problem for a system of the form: ![img](images/c03/inline226.gif), where the control domain is a subset U of ![img](images/c03/inline228.gif). Proposition 2 Consider an affine control system of the form

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0032.gif)

Then, outside the surface Σ: H[i] = 0, i = 1, . . ., m, the optimal solutions are given by the Hamiltonian ![img](images/c03/inline238.gif), where H[i] = p·F[i](x) is the Hamiltonian corresponding to the vector field F[i].

Proof We apply the maximum principle for the time-minimal control problem with control bound |u|≤1\. The pseudo-Hamiltonian takes the form H = H[0]+∑[i]u[i]H[i] and the control domain is the unit ball |u|≤1\. The maximization condition gives outside Σ: ![img](images/c03/inline245.gif). Plugging such control into H defines the normal hamiltonian H[r].

The solutions (x, p) of H[r] are called extremals of order zero and the surface Σ is called the switching surface. In order to be optimal, they have to satisfy H[r]≥0 and those where H[r] = 0 are called abnormal. In this case, the singular extremals belong to the surface Σ.

C. Geometric Aspects of the Optimal Control Theory

This chapter gives a heuristic introduction to the geometric tools of optimal control theory.

The goal of geometric optimal control theory is to analyze the trajectories of the Hamiltonian system given by the PMP from a geometric point of view in order to deduce the geometric properties of the extremals: smooth or broken, number of switchings![img](images/ctdot.gif) (a switching time is a time where the control field is discontinuous). An important point of this analysis is the fact that the PMP is only a necessary condition of optimality and one difficulty of the problem is to determine the cut point, that is, the first point along a given trajectory where the extremal ceases to be optimal. The computation of the cut point is a very difficult problem since it is a global problem, where all the Hamiltonian trajectories starting from the same initial point can play a role. In particular cases, one can determine geometrically the cut points that correspond to the points where two extremals with the same cost intersect. [Figure 6](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0006) displays this situation (see below for a more concrete example). The optimal control problem consists in minimizing the length of the trajectory going from A to the final point. In [Fig. 6](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0006), we consider two different optimal trajectories starting from point A. We can show that the red extremal is not optimal to go from A to D since in the BCD triangle, the BD length is smaller than the sum of the lengths BC and CD.

[Figure 6.](#chapter03.html__back42) Broken solution to go from A to D with the shortest length. The extremals in red and blue starting from the point A intersect in C with the same length. The red extremal is not optimal from the point C since one can construct a broken solution ABD with a shortest length. (See insert for color representation of the figure.)

![img](images/c03/nfg006.gif)

The computation of the cut points is global, but it is related to the computation of conjugate points. A conjugate point is a point where the extremal loses its local optimality when considering only extremals in a neighborhood of the first extremal (see [Fig. 9](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0009) for an illustration). From a physical point of view, a conjugate point corresponds to a point where the extremals starting from the same initial point concentrate in a neighborhood of the conjugate point. This concept is analogous to the concept of caustic in optics. A numerical algorithm detailed in Section II. E can be used to determine the position of the conjugate points. For a fixed initial condition, the set of cut and conjugate points are, respectively, called the cut and the conjugate loci.

The problems of quantum control that we consider in this chapter are related to the Grushin model on the sphere. This model is a Riemannian model defined by the metric g = dϕ²+tan²ϕdθ², where ϕ is the polar angle and θ the azimuthal angle of the spherical coordinates. Let x[0] and x[1] be two points on the sphere and γ a smooth path going from x[0] to x[1]. We assume that γ can be parameterized by two functions t![img](images/map.gif)ϕ(t) and t![img](images/map.gif)θ(t), where t![img](images/epsiv1.gif)[0, T]. The length L of the path given by the Grushin metric is equal to

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0033.gif)

Note that this length does not depend on the parameterization. Parameterizing this path by arclength, we minimize the time to go from x[0] to x[1] [14]. To parameterize a curve by arclength means that a new time τ, associated to the length of the curve, is defined as follows:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0034.gif)

and the cost is equal to ![img](images/c03/inline272.gif), where τ[f] is the control duration.

The metric g allows us to determine the length between two points on the sphere. Then, it defines an optimization problem if we look for the shortest path going from one point to the other.

One can also use another metric ![img](images/c03/inline275.gif) that is the restriction of the euclidian metric to the sphere. The metric ![img](images/c03/inline276.gif) can be written as:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0035.gif)

The cut and the conjugate loci can be determined for these two metrics. The results are represented in [Figs. 7](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0007) and [8](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0008). For the euclidian metric, the shortest path to join two points is an arc of a big circle. The first conjugate point that corresponds in this case to the cut point is reached when the length of the arc is equal to π and this point is the antipodal point of the initial point. This situation is not generic for the metrics on the sphere and it is related to the symmetries of the problem. The situation is more generic for the Grushin metric. In this case, the cut locus is a subset of the antipodal parallel of the initial point, as illustrated in [Fig. 8](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0008). The cut locus is not reduced to a point, except if the initial point is a pole. The conjugate and cut loci of a pole is the antipodal point for the Grushin metric. Note that this metric also has a singularity on the equator. The conjugate locus is plotted in blue in [Fig. 8](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0008). This locus has an astroid shape. Different extremals have been represented. They are tangent to the conjugate locus that is the envelope of these trajectories, leading thus to the concept of caustic.

[Figure 7.](#chapter03.html__back46) Extremals (in blue and red) and the corresponding cut point for the euclidian metric (top) and the Grushin one (bottom). The initial and the cut points are the points where the two extremals intersect. The cut locus is a subset of the green parallel for the Grushin metric. (See insert for color representation of the figure.)

![img](images/c03/nfg007.gif)

[Figure 8.](#chapter03.html__back47) Conjugate locus (in blue) and cut locus (in green) of the Grushin model on the sphere. The extremals are plotted in black. Their initial point is the point of coordinates (ϕ = π/4, θ = 0). The equations of the vertical and horizontal lines in red are ϕ = π/2 and θ = π. (See insert for color representation of the figure.)

![img](images/c03/nfg008.gif)

D. Indirect and Continuation Methods

In this section, we present some numerical methods to solve an optimal control problem.

We consider a problem with a given initial state x(0) and a target state x(T), where the control duration T can be fixed or not. Two types of numerical methods can be distinguished in optimal control to solve this problem, namely, the direct and the indirect methods. The direct approach consists in discretizing both the state of the system and the control, which reduces the optimal control problem to a nonlinear optimization problem (nonlinear programming). The indirect approach consists in solving, by a shooting method, a boundary problem given by the application of the PMP [28]. More precisely, this means that in the regular case one has to determine the initial adjoint state p(0) such that the corresponding Hamiltonian trajectory with initial conditions [x(0), p(0)] goes to x(T) at time T. This question can be very difficult to answer, in particular, if the dimension of the phase space is large. Another difficulty, which is not described here, is to take into account the singular extremals. The main drawback of indirect methods is their small domain of convergence with respect to the one of direct methods. They are, however, more accurate than the direct ones, which explains why there exist also hybrid approaches combining the advantages of the two methods. A geometric analysis of the optimal control problem can also be done in order to reduce the dimensionality of the problem, which simplifies the use of a shooting method. To summarize, if one has a sufficiently good approximation of p(0), one can use an indirect method and, for example, a standard Newton-type algorithm to solve the optimal control problem.

Another possibility is to use a continuation method. A smooth continuation method consists in the following. Suppose one wants to find a solution of a system of equations

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0036.gif)

where F is a smooth mapping. We consider a smooth homotopy path H(x, λ), such that H(x, 0)=G(x) and H(x, 1)=F(x), where G is a map having known zero points. In a smooth numerical continuation method (see [29]), the solutions are computed iteratively along this path using, for example, a Newton-type algorithm, the trial solutions at one step being given by the solutions of the previous step. This general method can be applied in Optimal Control. Indeed, as mentioned above, the optimal solution for a fixed set of boundary conditions can be computed by solving a shooting equation and the smooth continuation method can be applied if one can construct a smooth homotopy path between different optimal control problems. Different continuation methods exist in the literature, such as the discrete one which uses a Newton-type algorithm at each step, or the smooth one that uses the derivatives of the Hamiltonian trajectories along the homotopy path. An example will be given in Section IV for the optimal control of two-level dissipative quantum systems, where we illustrate the practical implementation of this algorithm.

E. Second-Order Optimality Conditions: The Concept of Conjugate Points

The second-order optimality conditions are used to determine the local optimality of extremal trajectories. By using the PMP for an open set U of values of the control fields, an optimal control has to satisfy the conditions ∂H/∂u = 0 and ∂²H/∂u²≤0, where H(x, p, u) is the pseudo-Hamiltonian. The condition on the second derivative corresponds to the local optimality of the control, that is, when one considers the extremals in a neighborhood of the reference trajectory (see [Fig. 9](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0009) for an illustration). Now, we assume that ∂²H/∂u²<0 along a given extremal z=(x, p). A conjugate point will be the first point of the trajectory for which this relation does not hold. A conjugate point is therefore the point where the trajectory ceases to be optimal. Some tools have to be introduced to compute the position of this conjugate point.

[Figure 9.](#chapter03.html__back44) Plot of the extremals in a neighborhood of a given extremal (large solid black curve)

![img](images/c03/nfg009.gif)

Locally, we can define a smooth function u(x, p) and plugging this function into the pseudo-Hamiltonian H gives the normal Hamiltonian H[r](x, p). The reference extremal is then solution of

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0037.gif)

Note: These equations are derived by using the fact that

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0038.gif)

and ∂H[r]/∂p=∂H/∂p. We introduce [Eq. (8)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0040)

[(8)](#chapter03.html__back53) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0039.gif)

which is called the Jacobi equation. Explicitly, this means that δz=(δx, δp) is solution of the system

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0040.gif)

with δx(0) = 0 and δp(0)=p[0]. A Jacobi field is a nontrivial solution of this equation. It is said to be vertical at time t if δx(t) = 0.

Next, we fix x[0] = x(0) and introduce the exponential mapping

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0041.gif)

Let L[0] be the space of points of the form {x[0], p}. The tangent space of L[0] is spanned by the n vertical Jacobi fields at time t = 0, since this space is locally diffeomorphic to ![img](images/c03/inline315.gif). If the final time is not fixed, the PMP yields the additional condition H = 0 and the dimension of this space is at most n−1 (see below for a concrete computation of the basis of this tangent space). We then introduce the space L[t] that is the image of L[0] at time t by the map ![img](images/c03/inline321.gif). The tangent space at time t of L[t] is spanned by the Jacobi fields image by the linearized system [(8)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0040) of the initial vertical Jacobi fields. A time t[c] is said to be conjugate if there exists a Jacobi field that is vertical at time t[c]. This is a precise formulation of the physical intuition of accumulation of trajectories at a conjugate point since for a vertical Jacobi field one has δx(t[c]) = 0. This behavior is analogous to the concept of caustics in optics. Under suitable assumptions, it can then be shown that the trajectory is locally optimal up to the first conjugate point [30]. A schematic description of the evolution of the set L[t] is represented in [Fig. 10](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0010). Using this idea, different numerical algorithms can be used to determine the position of conjugate points.

[Figure 10.](#chapter03.html__back55) Evolution of the set L[t] in dashed lines in the space (x, p). The solid black curve is an extremal trajectory of the system.

![img](images/c03/nfg010.gif)

Test for the Computation of Conjugate Times

In the energy minimization case, we provide a test to determine the position of the conjugate points.

One has to compute numerically the Jacobi fields J[i](t)=(δx[i](t), δp[i](t)) for i = 1, . . ., n with the initial conditions δx[i](0) = 0 and δp[i](0)=e[i], where the set (e[i])[i = 1,. . .,n] is a basis of ![img](images/c03/inline335.gif). Then, we calculate

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0042.gif)

and the rank is equal to n if t is not a conjugate time and lower or equal to n−1 for a conjugate time.

In the time-minimal case, one has to take into account the additional condition H = 0 and we can use three different tests. We introduce the set X of values of p[0] such that H[r](x[0], p[0]) is equal to 0\. The tangent space of L[0] that is a subset of X is locally diffeomorphic to ![img](images/c03/inline345.gif) and thus of dimension n−1.

In the first test, we consider the (n−1) dimensional vector space of Jacobi fields J[i](t)=(δx[i](t), δp[i](t)), i = 1, . . ., n−1, vertical at t = 0 and such that:

[(9)](#chapter03.html__back56) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0043.gif)

which ensures that δp[i](0) is tangent to the space X. Here, p(0) is the initial adjoint state of the extremal considered. The test consists in computing numerically the corresponding Jacobi fields and determining the conjugate time t[c] for which

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0044.gif)

A second test is to compute the n Jacobi fields J[i](t)=(δx[i](t), δp[i](t)), i = 1, . . ., n such that δp[i](0)=e[i], i = 1, . . ., n. In this case, the rank condition reads

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0045.gif)

for a conjugate time. In the third test, one uses the dynamics ![img](images/c03/inline357.gif) of the system. One considers n−1 Jacobi field such that the relations [(9)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0044) hold. At a conjugate point, the following determinant

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0046.gif)

is equal to 0.

Using these different tests, the numerical implementation of the computation of conjugate points is relatively straightforward. One has to integrate differential equations and to compute the rank or the determinant of a matrix. The singular value decomposition of this matrix is an efficient way to determine the variation of the rank of a matrix. The rank decreases by one when one of the singular values vanishes. Some examples will be given in Section IV for the time-optimal control of two-level dissipative quantum systems.

The different tests are implemented in the COTCOT software (Conditions of Order Two and COnjugates Times [30]) that can be downloaded free from the website of [31].

III. Application to the Control of a Three-Level Quantum System

The objective of this section is to apply the PMP to the control of a three-level quantum system by laser fields in a first part and then by laser fields plus von Neumann measurements. The results of this section are taken from [32].

A. Formulation of the Problem

We consider a three-level quantum system whose dynamics is governed by the Schrödinger equation. The system is described by a pure state |ψ(t)![img](images/rquo.gif) belonging to a three-dimensional (3D) Hilbert space ![img](images/c03/inline360.gif). The time evolution of |ψ(t)![img](images/rquo.gif) is given by

[(10)](#chapter03.html__back60) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0047.gif)

where H[0] is the field-free Hamiltonian defined in matrix form as:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0048.gif)

H[1] and H[2] read in the eigenbasis of H[0] as:

(11)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0049.gif)

where d is a real constant. [Equation (10)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0048) is written in units such that ![img](images/hbar.gif) = 1\. A basis of ![img](images/c03/inline360.gif) is given by the eigenvectors |1![img](images/rquo.gif), |2![img](images/rquo.gif), and |3![img](images/rquo.gif) of H[0]. Here, E[1](t) and E[2](t) are two real components of the electric field along two orthogonal directions of polarization. Both E[1](t) and E[2](t) are assumed to be in resonance with the frequency E[0]. In the rotating wave approximation [33], the equation for the time evolution of |ψ(t)![img](images/rquo.gif) can be written as:

[(12)](#chapter03.html__back62) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0050.gif)

where u is the complex Rabi frequency. In the interaction representation, [Eq. (12)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0051) becomes

[(13)](#chapter03.html__back64) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0051.gif)

where u[1] and u[2] are, respectively, the real and imaginary parts of the complex Rabi frequency. We keep the same notation for the state |ψ(t)![img](images/rquo.gif) after this transformation. The interaction representation here means that we have performed the unitary transformation U to the state |ψ(t)![img](images/rquo.gif):

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0052.gif)

Note that this transformation allows us to eliminate the drift term due to the field-free Hamiltonian H[0][14].

We denote by c[1], c[2], and c[3] the complex coefficients of the state |ψ(t)![img](images/rquo.gif) in the basis {|1![img](images/rquo.gif), |2![img](images/rquo.gif), |3![img](images/rquo.gif)}. We introduce the real coefficients x[i] (i![img](images/epsiv1.gif){1, 2, . . .6}) defined by

(14)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0053.gif)

Let x be the six-dimensional vector of coordinates x[i]. [Equation (13)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0052) reads in a more compact form as:

[(15)](#chapter03.html__back66) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0054.gif)

where F[1] and F[2] are the vector fields

(16)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0055.gif)

The dynamics takes place on a five-dimensional sphere S⁵ since ![img](images/c03/inline398.gif).

The interaction of the system with the laser field consists of a dipolar interaction with constant dipolar terms coupling only neighboring states. It is known that this type of system is not completely controllable by unitary evolution [32]. The noncomplete controllability of the system can be understood geometrically as follows: We define new coordinates X[i] by

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0056.gif)

For any choice of the controls u[1] and u[2] in [Eq. (15)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0055), only the coordinates {X[1], X[3], X[5]} and {X[2], X[4], X[6]} are coupled between each other. In addition, they fulfill the following relations

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0057.gif)

where R[i] and R[f] are two real constants, such that ![img](images/c03/inline406.gif). The system thus evolves on two spheres that we denote S[i] and S[f]. The radii R[i] and R[f] of the two spheres are determined from the initial state of the system and are constant for unitary evolution. To simplify the geometrical description of the control, we consider in this chapter that one of the radius is equal to one and the other to zero. In this case, we can assume that the state belongs only to one sphere since the dynamics on the other sphere is trivial. Using this description, one deduces that if the system is initially on one of the two spheres, then it will remain on this sphere by unitary evolution and it will not reach a state belonging, for example, to the other sphere. The description of the control is more difficult if the two radii are different from zero. In particular, due to the symmetry of [Eq. (15)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0055), the optimal trajectories on the two spheres are the same and cannot be controlled independently.

B. Optimal Control of a Three-Level Quantum System: The Grushin Model

In this section, we consider the optimal control on one of the two spheres. This corresponds to the optimal control problem associated with the Grusin model on the sphere [34], [14]. The geometric properties of this model were presented in Section II.C, but we show here that this problem can be completely solved analytically. We analyze the optimal control of this three-level system either with the constraint of minimizing the duration of the control or the energy of the laser field. We denote by ![img](images/c03/inline411.gif) the manifold of admissible values of the control fields. For the time minimum cost, we have the condition ![img](images/c03/inline412.gif) on the control field, whereas there is no restriction on laser fields if the cost minimizes the energy. The total duration T of the control is fixed for the energy cost problem.

The extremal trajectories, which are the same for H[E] and H[T], are represented in [Fig. 11](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0011) for a given value of j and different values of h. These trajectories have three symmetries. The figure is first symmetric with respect to translation in ϕ. In addition, two trajectories corresponding, respectively, to p[ϕ] and −p[ϕ] are symmetric with respect to the meridian ϕ = cst. Finally, two extremals starting from the same point (ϕ(0), θ(0)), with the same value of p[ϕ] but with opposite initial values of p[θ](0) intersect on the antipodal parallel θ = π−θ(0) with the same value of the cost. As shown in [Fig. 11](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0011), we notice that the trajectory with p[θ](0) = 0 is tangent to the antipodal parallel. [Figure 12](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0012) illustrates these two symmetries that can be determined by using the previous analytical computations. From geometric arguments of Section II.C, we recover also the notion of cut locus where two trajectories starting from the same initial point intersect with the same cost. The cut locus is here a subset of the antipodal parallel. Reference [35] gives a mathematical description of the cut locus for a class of metrics similar to the Grushin one on a two sphere.

C. Optimal Control of a Three-Level Quantum System by Laser Fields and von Neumann Measurements

Now, we describe the control assisted by von Neumann measurements (VNMs). The VNMs can be classified into two types: the instantaneous measurements and the continuous ones. Among instantaneous measurements, we also distinguish the selective ones where the state after the measurement is known (with a given probability) and the nonselective ones where this state is unknown [36]. Here, we will only consider selective instantaneous measurements. The question of the measurement driven quantum evolution has already been discussed in a series of work mainly from a numerical point of view (see [36-40] to cite a few), either with or without a laser field assisting the control. In this section, we revisit this problem by using the PMP and a more geometrical point of view.

We assume that the initial and the target states belong, respectively, to the two spheres S[i] and S[f]. We can choose, for example, |ψ[i]![img](images/rquo.gif)=|2![img](images/rquo.gif) and ![img](images/c03/inline502.gif). For the measurement process, the idea is to determine an observable Q for which the system passes from S[i] to S[f] after a measurement. We thus see that the introduction of measurements allows us to create a path from the initial state to the target state. This is possible if all the eigenvectors of Q belong to S[f] and form a basis of ![img](images/c03/inline508.gif). A possible choice is given by the vectors

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0090.gif)

The corresponding observable Q is equal to ![img](images/c03/inline510.gif), where the q[i]'s are real numbers. Let ![img](images/c03/inline512.gif) be the state of the system at time t. Then after the measurement, this state becomes |ϕ[i]![img](images/rquo.gif) (i![img](images/epsiv1.gif){1, 2, 3}) with the probability |a[i]|². Since all the eigenvectors of Q belong to S[f], the sphere of the target state, one sees that, whatever the result of the measurement, the target state can now be reached by unitary evolution. Note also that the control by laser field on the sphere S[f] will depend on the result of the measurement. We assume that the operator knows this result and can modify the control field according to the result of the measurement.

To solve this problem, we begin by the standard formulation of the PMP, (i.e., without measurement), as shown in Section III. B. Then, we assume that a measurement is performed at a time t![img](images/epsiv1.gif)[0, T]. The definition of the cost given below can be extended straightforwardly to the case of several measurements. We denote by |ψ(t)![img](images/rquo.gif) the state of the system at time t at which the measurement is performed. Since {ϕ[i]}[(i = 1,![img](images/ctdot.gif),3)] is a basis of ![img](images/c03/inline524.gif), |ψ(t)![img](images/rquo.gif) can be written as:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0091.gif)

Let C[0](t) be the cost corresponding to the optimal path from |ψ[i]![img](images/rquo.gif) to |ψ(t)![img](images/rquo.gif). Note that C[0] is equal to zero if t = 0\. We also introduce the costs C[i], (i = 1, . . ., 3) which are, respectively, associated to the optimal passage from |ϕ[i]![img](images/rquo.gif) to |ψ[f]![img](images/rquo.gif). The total cost of the control C(t) is then defined by

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0092.gif)

The choice of C(t) is related to the fact that the operator knows the result of the measurement and can modify the electric field accordingly. Here, ![img](images/c03/inline537.gif) can be viewed as an average of the three costs C[i]. From the cost C(t), the goal is then to determine the control fields, the observable Q and the time t at which the measurement is performed to minimize C(t). We will solve this problem by using twice the PMP: Once on S[i] and once on S[f]. Indeed, for a fixed observable Q, it is clear that the trajectories that minimize C correspond to the concatenation of extremal trajectories on S[i] and S[f]. We solve this optimal control problem in Section IV for particular examples. The general solution is very complex and can only be determined numerically.

To describe the dynamics of the system on the two spheres, we introduce two sets of spherical coordinates (θ[i], ϕ[i]) and (θ[f], ϕ[f]), such that

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0093.gif)

and

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0094.gif)

The solution of the optimal control problem on each sphere corresponds to the one of the Grusin model on the sphere.

We consider four different qualitative cases of control. We recall that the initial and target state belong, respectively, to S[i] and S[f].

*   Case (a): Passage from X[3] = 1 to X[4] = 1, that is, from state |2![img](images/rquo.gif) to state i|2![img](images/rquo.gif) (modification of the phase of the state |2![img](images/rquo.gif)). The measurement operator is not fixed but allows us to pass from S[i] to S[f].
*   Case (b): Passage from X[3] = 1 to X[2] = 1\. We assume that the operator Q is of the form

    ![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0095.gif)

    where α, β, and γ are real constants.

*   Case (c): Passage from a state of S[i] to the state (θ[f] = π/2, ϕ[f] = α), where α![img](images/epsiv1.gif)[0, 2π]. The states associated to the measurement are given by (θ[f] = π/4, ϕ[f] = 0), (θ[f] = 3π/4, ϕ[f] = 0), and (θ[f] = π/2, ϕ[f] = π/2). The angle α is chosen such that the cost to reach the target state from one of the three states of the measured observable is the same. In this symmetric case, the optimal trajectory does not depend on the initial state.
*   Case (d): Passage from |ψ[i]![img](images/rquo.gif)=|1![img](images/rquo.gif) to ![img](images/c03/inline574.gif), that is, (θ[f] = π/4, ϕ[f] = π/4). The three states associated with the measurement are (θ[f] = 0, ϕ[f] = 0), (θ[f] = π/2, ϕ[f] = 0), and (θ[f] = π/2, ϕ[f] = π/2).

Case (a)

We first solve the optimal control problem on S[i] starting from X[3] = 1\. This initial point is characterized by θ[0] = 0\. Using the results on the Grushin model, one deduces that the extremal trajectories are associated with the value of the conserved integral of motion j = 0\. The case θ[0] = 0 corresponds to a degenerate case that is not well defined if j≠0\. From [Eqs. (21)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0079), it is then straightforward to see that the optimal trajectories are lines of equation ϕ[i]=cst. The optimal synthesis, that is, all the extremals starting from the initial point, is represented in [Fig. 13](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0013). Using [Eqs. (13)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0077), one can determine the corresponding control fields ![img](images/c03/inline588.gif) and ![img](images/c03/inline589.gif), which are given by

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0096.gif)

for the time-minimum problem and by

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0097.gif)

where h[E]>0 for the energy minimization problem. Let (θ[i], ϕ[i]) be the coordinates of the final point on S[i]. For the minimization of the time, since ![img](images/c03/inline593.gif), one deduces that C[T,0] = θ[i]. For the energy case, we have

![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0098.gif)

But since ![img](images/c03/inline595.gif), this leads to ![img](images/c03/inline596.gif).

To summarize, the preceding computation gives the cost to reach a point of S[i] from X[3] = 1\. The next step is to apply a measurement to the system. The measurement is associated with the observable Q that reads

We assume that the coordinates of the three states |ϕ[1]![img](images/rquo.gif), |ϕ[2]![img](images/rquo.gif), and |ϕ[3]![img](images/rquo.gif) can be written, respectively, as (θ[m], ϕ[m]), (θ[m]+π/2, ϕ[m]) and (π/2, ϕ[m]−π/2), where (θ[m], ϕ[m]) are coordinates on S[f]. The last step of the optimization procedure is to minimize C[T] and C[E] as a function of (θ[i], ϕ[i]) (i.e., the choice of the time of the measurement) and (θ[m], ϕ[m]) (choice of the observable Q). Since C[T,E]≥0, one sees that the optimal choice corresponds to θ[i] = θ[m] = 0, which leads to C[T,E] = 0\. To determine a solution that is not trivial, we fix the observable Q, that is, the coordinates θ[m] and ϕ[m], and we search for the values of θ[i] and ϕ[i] that minimize the cost. The two computations being similar, we only consider the time-minimum control problem.
