IV. Application to the Time-Optimal Control of Two-Level Dissipative Quantum Systems

The objective of this section is to describe the geometric aspects of the optimal control of two-level dissipative quantum systems. When a quantum system interacts with a Markovian or a non-markovian bath, the control field cannot generally fully compensate the dissipation effect that largely enhances the difficulty of the control. This point has been rigourously shown in [21] for a dynamics governed by the Lindblad equation [41-43]. In this context, several studies using numerical optimization techniques have proved that efficient control can still be achieved (see [44-50] to cite a few). We propose here to give a geometric analysis of this question in the time-minimal case, the maximum of the control field being fixed to an arbitrary value. Note that this cost functional is particularly relevant in the context of a dissipative environment especially when the dissipation effect is undesirable to reach the target state.

A. The Kossakowski–Lindblad Equation for N-Level Dissipative Quantum Systems

First, we recall the form of the Kossakowski–Lindblad equation for an N-level quantum system described by a density operator ρ[41-52]. The dynamics of ρ is governed by the following first-order differential equation:

(34)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0115.gif)

This equation differs from the standard von Neumann equation ![img](images/c03/inline744.gif) in that a dissipation operator L[D] acting on the set of density operators has been added. This linear operator, describes the interaction with the environment, cannot be chosen arbitrarily. Under particular assumptions, such as the semigroup dynamics, the norm continuity, and the conservation of probability [41, 42], the form of L[D] can be deduced from a rigorous mathematical analysis. The dissipation operator L[D] can be written as follows:

(35)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0116.gif)

where the L[k] are arbitrary N×N matrices. The operator L[D] can also be given in the Kossakowski form:

(36)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0117.gif)

where the matrices V[k], k = 1, . . ., N²−1 are trace-zero and orthonormal. The product of two matrices A and B is defined by Tr(A†B). In this form, the coefficient a[kk'] has to be positive to ensure the complete positivity of the density matrix. A particular choice for the matrices V[k] is the following. We introduce the coherence vector that is a basis for the space of density matrices given by [23, 25]:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0118.gif)

for 1≤r≤N−1 and r<s≤N and together with ![img](images/c03/inline760.gif). In these equations, e[r,s] is the operator |r![img](images/rquo.gif)![img](images/lquo.gif)s| and I[N] is the identity matrix of dimension N. Note the similitude with the Pauli matrices that are elements of this basis for N = 2. After a straightforward change of the indices, any density operator can be written as:

(37)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0119.gif)

where s[k] are real coefficients. The matrices V[k] can be chosen as the elements of the coherence vector for k≥1.

We now explain the physical interpretation of the different operators L[k] of the Lindblad equation, which can be expressed as a function of the physical relaxation parameters describing the interaction with the environment. In this paragraph, we describe this point for two-level systems. We represent the density operator by a 2×2 matrix

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0120.gif)

We denote by H the Hamiltonian of the two-level system without the dissipative part. We choose the following generators:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0121.gif)

which describe elementary physical processes associated with dissipation. They also represent generators of the Lindblad equation for the two-level quantum system. The parameters Γ[1], Γ[2], and Γ[3] are real positive constants. Here, L[1] is the generator of the transition from the excited state |2![img](images/rquo.gif) to the ground state |1![img](images/rquo.gif) that can be interpreted as associated with spontaneous emission. The parameter L[2] describes the reverse process, that is, the passage |1![img](images/rquo.gif) to |2![img](images/rquo.gif) with absorption of energy. Here, L[3] represents pure dephasing processes [51].

A straightforward calculation leads to the equations

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0122.gif)

If we set γ[12]=Γ[1], γ[21]=Γ[2], and ![img](images/c03/inline784.gif), we can write

(38)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0123.gif)

which is the Redfield representation of the Lindblad equation. Finally, we notice that the positivity of the real coefficients Γ[1], Γ[2], and Γ[3] induces the following condition

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0124.gif)

If one were to take in the Redfield equations, the coefficients Γ, γ[12] and γ[21] that do not satisfy this constraint then the evolution of ρ would not preserve the positivity [22].

The Redfield form of the Lindblad equation can also be written for an N−level quantum system. The diagonal elements of the density matrix satisfy:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0125.gif)

where 1≤n≤N and 1≤k≤N. The parameter γ[kn] is the relaxation rate from state |k![img](images/rquo.gif) to state |n![img](images/rquo.gif), which means that γ[kn]ρ[kk] represents the population loss. The dynamics of the off-diagonal elements of the density matrix is governed by the following differential equation:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0126.gif)

where Γ[kn] is the dephasing rate between the states |k![img](images/rquo.gif) and |n![img](images/rquo.gif). A part of the dephasing is due to the relaxation rate, but other sources may exist. The total dephasing rate can be written as follows:

![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0127.gif)

where the term ![img](images/c03/inline802.gif) is the contribution of the relaxation to the dephasing rate and the term ![img](images/c03/inline803.gif) the contribution of other sources called pure dephasing rate. Note that the Lindblad equation imposes nontrivial constraints on the different dissipation parameters of the Redfield equation (see [22] for a derivation for three and four level quantum systems).

B. Construction of the Model

In this section, we discuss the last step of the construction of the model. We consider the control of a two-level dissipative quantum system whose dynamics is governed by the Lindblad equation. The evolution equation can be written as:

(39)![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0128.gif)

where H[0] is the field-free Hamiltonian of the system, H[1] represents the interaction with the control field and ![img](images/c03/inline806.gif) is the dissipative part of the equation. Here, H[1] is assumed to be of the form

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0129.gif)

where the operators μ[x] and μ[y] are proportional to the Pauli matrices σ[x] and σ[y] in the eigenbasis of H[0]. The electric field is the superposition of two linearly polarized fields E[x] and E[y]. These two fields are in resonance with the Bohr frequency E[2]−E[1]. Also, this Hamiltonian describes the dynamics of a spin 1/2 particle in a magnetic field. In this case, the term H[0] corresponds to a constant magnetic field along the z-axis and the dynamics is controlled by two magnetic fields polarized, respectively, along the x-and y-axes.

In the rotating wave approximation [33], the time evolution of ρ(t) satisfies the following Redfield form of the Kossakowski–Lindblad equation

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0130.gif)

where u is the complex Rabi frequency of the laser field (the real and imaginary parts are the amplitudes of the real fields E[x] and E[y]) and γ[12], γ[21], and Γ are positive real constants describing the interaction with the environment. In this equation, ω is the energy difference between the ground and excited states and the frequency of the laser field. In the interaction representation, this equation becomes

[(40)](#chapter03.html__back123) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0131.gif)

Here, the interaction representation means that we have performed the unitary transformation U to the mixed-state ρ:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0132.gif)

Since Tr[ρ] = 1, the density matrix ρ depends on three real parameters that can be given by the coordinates of the Bloch ball: ![img](images/c03/inline832.gif), y = 2ℑ[ρ[12]], and z = ρ[22]−ρ[11]. From [(40)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0132), one deduces that these coordinates satisfy the following system of inhomogeneous linear differential equations:

[(41)](#chapter03.html__back125) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0133.gif)

u[1] and u[2] being two real functions such that u = u[1]+iu[2]. The dynamics is called either unital if γ[12] = γ[21], that is, the fixed point of the free dynamics is the center of the Bloch ball or affine otherwise [21]. [Equations (41)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0134) can be written in a more compact form

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0134.gif)

with the vector x of coordinates (x, y, z) and the three vector fields F[0], F[1], and F[2] defined by

C. Geometric Analysis of Lindblad Equation

The objective of this section is to present control techniques that can be used to analyze finite-dimensional quantum systems whose dynamics is governed by the Lindblad equation. In particular, our goal is to solve the control problem associated with the two-level dissipative quantum system modeled in Section IV.B [[Eq. (41)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0134)]. We consider the time-optimal problem with constraint ![img](images/c03/inline844.gif), which is not restrictive up to a rescaling of the dissipative parameters. The energy minimization control problem can be analyzed along the same lines and shares similar properties. First, we give some general results to present the geometric framework.

The sign of ![img](images/c03/inline860.gif) is given by p[θ] and if p[θ] = 0, then θ is constant. The extremal synthesis is thus for a point on the z-axis (up to a rotation) given by the synthesis in the plane θ = 0 and up to a rotation u can be restricted to the single input control (u[1], 0).

In conclusion, the synthesis in a meridian plane is associated with the 2D-system assuming the laser field real. Moreover, the analysis is clearly simplified using the spherical coordinates that are the geometric coordinates adapted to the problem.

In this case, this means that the dynamics on the sphere is described by the Grushin model, while the radial coordinate is not coupled to the two-angle coordinates. 3\. The Optimal Control Problem

In this section, we analyze the singularities of the Hamiltonian system in order to deduce the properties of the optimal trajectories. We obtain that every optimal trajectory can be of two types.

*   In the first case, the trajectory is external with p[θ] = 0, contained in a meridian plane, which is a time-optimal solution of the 2D system, where x=(0, y, z) and u=(u[1], 0).
*   In the second case, the trajectory is the connection of smooth extremal arcs, which are solutions of the Hamiltonian H with p[θ]≠0, while the only possible connections are located in the equatorial plane ϕ = π/2.

Some work has to be done to obtain a regularity result about optimal trajectories. This is due to the existence of a switching surface Σ:P[1] = P[2] = 0 in which we can connect two extremal arcs, provided we respect the Erdmann–Weierstrass conditions at the junction, that is, the adjoint vector remains continuous and the Hamiltonian is constant [53, 54]. The set Σ can also contain singular arcs for which P[1] = P[2] = 0 holds identically. Hence, we can have intricate behaviors for such systems. In our case, the situation is simplified by the symmetry of revolution. Indeed, if p[θ] = 0 then the singularities are related to the classification of extremals in the single-control case. We cannot connect an extremal with p[θ]≠0 to an extremal, where p[θ] = 0, since the adjoint vector has to be continuous. Hence, the only remaining possibility is to connect two extremals with p[θ]≠0 at a point of Σ leading to the conditions p[ϕ] = 0 and p[θ]cotϕ = 0 in spherical coordinates. Since p[θ]≠0, one gets ϕ = π/2\. The result is proved.

From this preliminary analysis, we observe that the time-minimal control problem can be analyzed in two steps. For physical reasons, the initial condition is taken as a pure state that is on the Bloch sphere.

*   Initial condition on the (Oz)-axis: In this case, using the symmetry group, the extremal synthesis is equivalent up to a rotation around (Oz) to the one of a planar system on x = 0 with u[2] = 0 and |u[1]|≤1\. We use standard techniques [9] and the main features of the syntheses are described in Section IV.D.
*   Initial condition not on the (Oz)-axis: We start the analysis from the case Γ=γ[+] and γ[−] = 0, which is a singular case where the purity r of the system is not controllable and the time minimal analysis corresponds precisely to the Grushin case on the two sphere. Then, we deform this system in order to deduce the properties of the model in the general case. We show in particular that the Hamiltonian system is integrable for γ[−] = 0, which allows us to analytically solve the optimal control problem. In the case, γ[−]≠0, we use a numerical analysis to conclude.

D. Time-Optimal Control of Two-Level Dissipative Quantum Systems: The Single Control Case

1\. Introduction

Here, we consider the case where the initial condition is on the axis (Oz). As stated in Section IV.C.3, the control problem can then be restricted to a single-input planar control with, for example, u[2] = 0, |u[1]|≤1, and x = 0\. In this section, we construct the optimal synthesis for different values of the dissipative parameters. The core of the results presented here are contained in [55].

The system [(41)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0134) becomes

and

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0168.gif)

with the restriction that |z|≤1\. In the case Γ=γ[+], S is only composed of the vertical line of equation y = 0\. The corresponding singular control u[s] is given by

[(46)](#chapter03.html__back144a) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0169.gif)

We recall that u[s] can be calculated as a feedback control by imposing that the second derivative of Φ with respect to time is equal to 0:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0170.gif)

For the line y = 0 of S, this leads to u[s](x) = 0\. For the line z=[−γ[−]/2(Γ−γ[+]),] we obtain

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0171.gif)

The control is admissible here if |u[s](x)|≤1 implies the condition

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0172.gif)

Finally, we introduce the collinear setC that is the set of points where F and G are collinear:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0173.gif)

In our case study, simple algebra leads to

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0174.gif)

In the case γ[−]≠0, C is therefore the union of two parabolas. This set is either above or below the line z=[−γ[−]/2Γ−2γ[+]] according to the signs of γ[−] and of Γ−γ[+]. For γ[−] = 0, this set is reduced to the origin (y = 0, z = 0). The set C has the following physical interpretation. The purity of a quantum state is here defined by the function 2Tr[ρ²]−1=y²+z². The set C divides the plane (y, z) into a region where the purity of the state locally increases and a region where it locally decreases. On the boundary, the purity is preserved. This point can be understood as follows. The conservative vector field G is orthoradial for each point (y, z)≠(0, 0) of the manifold. The vector field F does not modify the purity of the state if the radial component of F vanishes, that is, if F is parallel to G, which is the definition of C. Next, we analyze the fixed points of the dynamics when the field is on, which are defined by F+uG = 0\. Since F and G are parallel, the fixed points belong to C. Inversely, one can ask if every point of C corresponds to a limit point of the dynamics. The answer is positive for a real nonbounded control u since the limits can be written

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0175.gif)

The set C and S are represented in [Fig. 17](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0017) for particular values of the dissipative parameters.

[Figure 17.](#chapter03.html__back136) Division of ![img](images/c03/inline1601.gif) by the sets C (in solid line) and S (in dashed and dot–dashed lines) for γ[−]≠0\. The dashed and dot–dashed lines represent, respectively, antiturnpike and turnpike arcs (see text). The exterior circle in the solid line corresponds to the limit of the Bloch ball in the plane (y, z). The small open circle indicates the position of the fixed–point of the free dynamics. Numerical values are taken to be Γ = 1, γ[−]=−0.2 and γ[+] = 0.4.

![img](images/c03/nfg017.gif)

On the complementary of C, we can define the clock form that will be used in the construction of the optimal synthesis.

The Clock Form

In this section, we derive the expression of the clock form is denoted α [8]. By definition, the clock form is a 1-form that fulfills the following conditions:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0176.gif)

A solution of this system exists except on the set C, where F and G are collinear. If we write α as α = α[y]dy+α[z]dz, then simple algebra shows that α[y] and α[z] are solutions of the system

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0177.gif)

We obtain

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0178.gif)

From the 1-form α, we can define the 2-form dα that is given by

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0179.gif)

and reads after some calculations

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0180.gif)

If we write dα as ![img](images/c03/inlinea601.gif), then one sees that g(y, z) = 0 on S and that the function g has a constant sign in the regions delimited by the lines of S. This point is displayed in [Fig. 18](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0018) for a given set of dissipative parameters.

[Figure 18.](#chapter03.html__back138) Sign of the function

g in the plane (y, z) for Γ = 3, γ[+] = 0.4, and γ[−]=−0.2\. The large solid lines indicate the position of the set S.![img](images/c03/nfg018.gif)

As suggested by its name, the clock form is a form that allows one to determine the time taken to travel a path and to compare the extremals. Let γ be a path in the plane (y, z), which does not cross C and T the time of travel along γ. We have

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0181.gif)

Now, we consider two paths γ[1] and γ[2] starting and ending at the same points and, respectively, associated with the durations T[1] and T[2]. One shows by using the Stokes theorem that

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0182.gif)

where D is the surface delimited by γ[1]∪−γ[2]. For paths γ[1] and γ[2] that lie in one of the four quadrants defined by S, we can straightforwardly determine the time-optimal trajectory.

Turnpike and Antiturnpike Curves

Not every trajectory lying in the set S can be an optimal trajectory. In order to characterize this point, we introduce the notion of turnpike and antiturnpike curves [9]. Let x∉S∪C and the function f(x)=−Δ[S](x)/Δ[C](x). A turnpike curve is an arc lying in S∪C^c such that for every point x of this arc X(x) and Y(x) are not tangent to S. It is also assumed that X(x) and Y(x) point to opposite sides of S that define two regions Ω[x] and Ω[y]. If f(x)>0 (resp. f(x)<0) on Ω[y] and f(x)<0 (respectively f(x)>0) on Ω[x] then the arc is a turnpike (resp. antiturnpike) arc. The relation with the optimal synthesis can be stated as follows: Using the clock form α, it can be shown that the antiturnpike trajectories are not optimal. [Figure 17](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0017) displays the turnpike and the antiturnpike curves for particular values of the dissipative parameters.

3\. Four Different Illustrative Examples

We consider four different qualitative cases of control that allow us to study several physically relevant situations. A pure state is characterized by y²+z² = 1 and a mixed state by y²+z²≤1.

*   Case (a): Conversion of a pure state into a mixed state with (γ[−]/γ[+] = 0, Γ>γ[+]+2).
*   Case (b): Conversion of a pure state into a mixed state with (γ[−]/γ[+] = 0, γ[+]−2<Γ<γ[+]+2).
*   Case (c): Purification of the completely random mixed state that corresponds to the center of the Bloch ball (γ[−]/γ[+]=−1, Γ>γ[+]+2).
*   Case (d): Conversion of a pure state into a mixed state with (γ[−]/γ[+]=−0.5, Γ>γ[+]+2).

The numerical values we have chosen for illustrations are given in [Table I](#chapter03.html_c03-tbl-0001)

[Table I](#chapter03.html__back141) Numerical Values of the Dissipative Constants in Arbitrary Units.

![img](images/c03/table_image_001.gif)

Some comments can be made on the choice of the dissipative parameters. This choice both depends on the structure of the sets C and S and on the characteristics of two particular extremals denoted X[−] and Y[−] that start at the initial point, and correspond, respectively, to a constant control equal to -1 and 1\. Here, X[−] and Y[−] trajectories are either pseudo-periodic or aperiodic according to the sign of the discriminant Δ=(Γ−γ[+])²−4 of the system

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0183.gif)

An exact resolution of the dynamics shows that the trajectory of the system is aperiodic if Δ>0 and pseudo-periodic otherwise. In [Table I](#chapter03.html_c03-tbl-0001), we have chosen for three of the four examples Γ such that Γ>γ[+]+2 to simplify the local structure of the optimal synthesis around the fixed point of the dynamics. A pseudo-periodic trajectory is locally a spiral in the plane (y, z) around this fixed point that makes the analysis more complex.

Case (a)

Proposition 4 In case (a), each optimal trajectory is bang–bang and has at most one switching, that is, of the form γ [−] γ[+] or γ[+]γ[−]. An arc of the sequence can be empty.

Proof We use the clock form α and the symmetry of the diagram with respect to the line y = 0 to conclude. Note that the clock form can only be used for trajectories belonging to one of the four quadrants defined by S and that do not cross C. The singular line y = 0 is not optimal since this line is an antiturnpike curve. ![img](images/squ.gif)

[Figure 19](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0019) displays the optimal synthesis for this problem.

[Figure 19.](#chapter03.html__back143) Optimal synthesis for case (a). The dashed line indicates the locus S and the small open circle indicates the fixed point of the dynamics.

![img](images/c03/nfg019.gif)

Case (b)

The situation is a little more complex in case (b). For z>0, the synthesis is similar to case (a), that is, the trajectories are either bang or bang-bang.

Proposition 5 In case (b), optimal controls are of the form γ[±]γ[s]γ[±]. The singular control is u[s] = 0.

Proof The trajectories that do not cross the line z = 0 are bang or bang–bang and optimal up to the line y = 0\. A singular control along the horizontal line of S is optimal. Since γ[−] = 0, from [(46)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0170) one deduces that the singular control is given by u[s] = 0 on the line z = 0\. From this singular line originate optimal trajectories with control u=±1\. Using the clock form α and the symmetries of the diagram, it can be shown that these trajectories cannot switch again. ![img](images/squ.gif)

This optimal synthesis is represented in [Fig. 20](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0020).

[Figure 20.](#chapter03.html__back145) Same as [Fig. 19](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0019) but for case (b). The dotted–dashed line represents the singular trajectory S. The small insert is a zoom of the optimal synthesis near the origin.

![img](images/c03/nfg020.gif)

Case (c)

Proposition 6 Optimal trajectories are of the form γ[±]γ[s]. The singular control is u[s] = 0.

Proof The singular line y = 0, z<0 is optimal since this line is a turnpike curve. From [(46)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0170), one sees that the corresponding singular control is zero. Switches can occur from the initial X[−] and Y[−] trajectories, but they do not lead to optimal trajectories. Inversely, X[−] and Y[−] trajectories originating from S are found to be optimal. When two extremals cross C, α cannot be used and a direct numerical comparison is then performed. The switching function Φ tells us that these curves do not switch. ![img](images/squ.gif)

[Figure 21](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0021) displays the optimal synthesis for this problem.

[Figure 21.](#chapter03.html__back147) Same as [Fig. 19](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0019), but for the case (c)

![img](images/c03/nfg021.gif)

Case (d)

Case (d) is more complex and corresponds roughly to the composition of cases (b) and (c). The difficulty lies in the global structure of the control or, in other words, in gluing the two preceding local analysis. For z>0, the optimal synthesis is similar to the cases (a) or (b) with bang or bang–bang trajectories. The bottom of the optimal synthesis from the point of intersection of the initial X[−] and Y[−] trajectories is similar to case (c). Now, we describe the central part of the synthesis

Proposition 7 Near the origin, the optimal trajectories are bang-bang and pass through a switch curve C[S].

Proof The horizontal singular line of S does not correspond to a singular trajectory since |u[s](x)|>1, which is a nonadmissible control. We consider the first points of intersection of S with the initial X− and Y− trajectories (see [Fig. 22](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0022)). A switch curve denoted C[S] originates from this point. We determine numerically the exact locus of C[S] by using the switching function. Here, C[S], C, and S intersect at the origin. Since the line y = 0 is turnpike for γ[−]/γ[+]<z<0, we can ask if this singular trajectory is optimal that is, if we can have a local optimal synthesis of the form given by [Fig. 23](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0023). To answer this question, we use the switching function Φ. For x(t)![img](images/epsiv1.gif)C[S]∪S, Φ(t) = 0, that is, the vectors p(t) and G(x(t)) are orthogonal. Since the direction of G is known (G is orthoradial), one can deduce the direction of p(t). Let z[1] and z[2] be two points belonging, respectively, to C[S] and S. The vectors G(z) associated to these points are schematically represented in [Fig. 23](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0023). Now, we let the states z[1] and z[2] go to (0, 0) and we determine the directions of the different adjoint states. We recall that the PMP states that p is a continuous function that does not vanish. When z[1] goes to (0, 0), one deduces by a continuity argument that p[1] is vertical in O. When z[2] goes to (0, 0), the limit direction of p[2] is given by the switch curve C[S]. To respect the continuity of p, one sees that C[S] has to be tangent to the line y = 0 in O. Due to the complexity of analytical calculations, we have checked numerically that this is not the case. The singular line for z<0 is therefore not optimal.![img](images/squ.gif)

[Figure 22.](#chapter03.html__back148) Same as [Fig. 19](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0019) but for the case (d). The dotted line represents the switch curve C[S].

![img](images/c03/nfg022.gif)

[Figure 23.](#chapter03.html__back149) Possible optimal synthesis around the origin.

![img](images/c03/nfg023.gif)

In addition, when the initial X[−] and Y[−] trajectories cross C, the angle between the vectors F(x) and G(x) changes its sign. New optimal trajectories originate from this point of intersection and correspond to two new regions of the reachable set. The optimal synthesis is represented in [Fig. 22](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0022).

Physical Interpretation

From the results obtained in the preceding paragraphs, some qualitative conclusions can be made with respect to the dissipation effect on the time optimal control of the dynamics. The dissipation is not undesirable when the dissipation allows us to purify the system [cases (c) and (d)] and help accelerating the control. In contrast, for the conversion of a pure state into a mixed state, the dissipation alone increases the duration of the control and its effect is not beneficial for the control [cases (a) and (b)]. Example (d) summarizes the situation well. As long as the purity of the state decreases, it is advantageous to use a control field, but when the purity starts increasing the dissipation alone becomes more efficient.

E. Application to the Optimal Control in Nuclear Magnetic Resonance

One of the most promising fields of applications of geometric optimal control in quantum control is the control of spin systems in NMR [13, 56]. Here, we apply the previous computations to a physical example, namely, the optimal control by a magnetic field of a spin 1/2 particle in interaction with an environment [13, 17]. To simplify the discussion, we assume that the frequency of the control field is resonant with the transition frequency of the spin [15]. The dynamics of the spin 1/2 particle is governed by the Bloch equation:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0184.gif)

where ![img](images/c03/inline1145.gif) is the magnetization vector and ![img](images/c03/inline1146.gif) is the equilibrium point of the dynamics. We assume that the control field ![img](images/c03/inline1147.gif) satisfies the constraint ![img](images/c03/inline1148.gif). We introduce the normalized coordinates ![img](images/c03/inline1149.gif), which implies that at thermal equilibrium the z component of the scaled vector ![img](images/c03/inline1151.gif) is by definition +1\. The normalized control field which satisfies |u|≤2π is defined as ![img](images/c03/inline1152.gif), while the normalized time τ is given by τ=(ω[max]/2π)t. Dividing the previous system by ω[max]M[0]/(2π), one deduces that the dynamics of the normalized coordinates is ruled by the following system of differential equations:

![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0185.gif)

where Γ = 2π/(ω[max]T[2]) and γ = 2π/(ω[max]T[1]).

We consider the control problem of bringing the system from the equilibrium point ![img](images/c03/inline1160.gif) to the zero-magnetization point that is the center of the Bloch ball. Since the initial point belongs to the z-axis, the controlled system is equivalent to a single-input system, where, for example, u[y] = 0. Taking u[y] = 0, we are thus considering a single-input problem in a plane of the form:

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0186.gif)

where the subscript x has been omitted for the control parameter. We can then apply, the theoretical description of the previous paragraph where F=(−Γy, γ−γz) and G=(−z, y).

We introduce the switching function Φ=−p[y]z+p[z]y[9]. In this case, the set S corresponds to the union of the vertical line y = 0 and of the horizontal one with z given by

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0187.gif)

if Γ≠γ (or equivalently if T[1]≠T[2]). The corresponding singular control u[s] is given by

(47) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0188.gif)

One deduces that the singular control vanishes on the vertical singular line and that it is admissible, that is, |u[s]|≤2π, on the horizontal one if |y|≥|γ(γ−2Γ)|/[2π(2Γ−2γ)]. For smaller values of y, the system cannot follow the horizontal singular arc and a switching curve appears from the point where the admissibility is lost [9]. The optimality of the singular trajectories can be determined geometrically by using the clock form. It can be shown that the horizontal singular line is locally optimal and that the vertical one is optimal if z>z[0].

We consider the control problems defined by the relaxation parameters γ^(−1) and Γ^(−1) (expressed in the normalized time unit defined above) of 23.9 and 1.94, respectively, and M[0]≈2.15×10^(−5). We compare the optimal control law with an intuitive one used in NMR. The intuitive solution is composed of a bang pulse to reach the opposite point of the initial state along the z-axis followed by a zero control, where we let the dissipation act up to the center of the Bloch ball. The optimal and the intuitive solutions are plotted in [Fig. 24](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0024). Geometric tools allow us to show that the optimal control is the concatenation of a bang pulse, followed successively by a singular control along the horizontal singular line, another bang pulse and a zero singular control along the vertical singular line. [Figure 24](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0024) also displays the switching curve that has been determined numerically by considering a series of trajectories with u=+2π originating from the horizontal singular set, where ϕ = 0. The points of the switching curve correspond to the first point of each trajectory where the switching function vanishes. To determine the optimal control law, we have also checked that the second bang pulse of the optimal sequence does not cross the switching curve up to the vertical singular axis. In this example, a gain of 58% is obtained for the optimal solution over the intuitive one, which shows the interest of geometric techniques.

F. Time-Optimal Control of Two-Level Dissipative Quantum Systems: The Double-Input Case

In this section, we study the optimal synthesis for the double-input case, that is, for the general case where two control fields are considered. The discussion is splitted into two cases: The case γ[−] = 0, which is the situation, where the extremal flow is integrable and the generic case, if γ[−]≠0\. A more mathematical presentation of this question can be found in [53, 54].

1\. The Integrable Case

For γ[−] = 0, the Hamiltonian H reads

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0189.gif)

Some properties of H are invariant when γ[+] and Γ vary.

Proposition 8 The Hamiltonian H is integrable.

Proof Using the change of coordinates ρ=lnr, H can be written

![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0190.gif)

where p[ρ] is the momentum conjugate to the coordinate ρ. The Hamiltonian flow defined by H is integrable since p[θ] and p[ρ] are constants of the motion.![img](images/squ.gif)

The case |Γ−γ[+]|<2

Now, we give the main result of this section that is established for |γ[+]−Γ|<2.

Proposition 9 For p[ρ] and p[θ]>0 fixed, there exist two trajectories starting from (r(0), ϕ(0), θ(0)), which intersect with the same cost at a point such that ϕ = π−ϕ(0).

Note that the symmetry of the flow of H with respect to the meridian is preserved. More precisely, if p[θ]→−p[θ], then we have two extremals with the same length symmetric with respect to the meridian, so we can assume p[θ]>0 in proposition 9\. Some lemmas are required before the proof of proposition 9.

Lemma 1 The derivative dϕ/dθ along an extremal for H = h is given by

![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0191.gif)

where Δ is equal to

![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0192.gif)

Proof Using the system [(42)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0042), we have

![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0193.gif)

and

![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0194.gif)

One deduces that p[ϕ] is solution of the following second-order polynomial equation:

![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0195.gif)

The solutions ![img](images/c03/inline1215.gif) of this equation reads

[(49)](#chapter03.html__back165) ![equation](images/c03/c03-mdis-0196.gif)

and a simple calculation completes the proof. ![img](images/squ.gif)

Using lemma 4.1, we notice that for h, p[ρ], and p[θ] fixed, the derivative dϕ/dθ along an extremal only depends (up to a sign) on ϕ.

Lemma 2 The equation Δ(ϕ) = 0 has two solutions in the interval]0, π[.

Proof The equation Δ(ϕ) = 0 is equivalent to the equation

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0197.gif)

which has two solutions ϕ[0] and π−ϕ[0] in the interval]0, π[when |γ[+]−Γ| <2\. ![img](images/squ.gif)

Having established lemmas 1 and 2, we can pass to the proof of proposition 9.

Proof We fix h, p[ρ], and p[θ]>0 and we consider the projection of the corresponding extremal curves on the unit sphere in coordinates (ϕ, θ). These projections are viewed as functions of the form ϕ[θ]:θ![img](images/map.gif)ϕ(θ) whose derivative is given by lemma 4.1\. We use lemma 4.2 and without loss of generality we assume that 0<ϕ[0]<π/2. A simple examination of Δ leads to the fact that the function ϕ[θ] reaches, respectively, for the first time its minimum and its maximum in θ = θ[−] and θ = θ[+] such that ϕ[θ](θ[−])=ϕ[0] and ϕ[θ](θ[+])=π−ϕ[0]. Note also that the sign of the derivative dϕ/dθ is constant for θ≠θ[−] and θ≠θ[+]. One finally deduces that the function ϕ[θ] is periodic of period 2|θ[−]−θ[+]|. An example of such curves is displayed in [Fig. 25](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0025).

The corresponding control laws are represented in (b). In (a), the small insert represents a zoom of the optimal trajectory near the origin. The dotted line is the switching curve originating from the horizontal singular line. The vertical dashed line corresponds to the intuitive solution. The solid green curve is the optimal trajectory near the origin. (See insert for color representation of the figure.)

![img](images/c03/nfg024.gif)

Other parameters are taken to be p[ϕ](0)=−1 and 2.33, ϕ(0)=π/4, p[r](0)=r(0) = 1 and p[θ] = 2\. Dashed lines represent the equator and the antipodal parallel located at ϕ = 3π/4\. Vertical solid lines correspond to the angles θ[−] and θ[+] for one of the two extremals.

![img](images/c03/nfg025.gif)

To determine the position of the intersection, we now consider the two extremal curves starting from the same point (r(0), ϕ(0), θ(0) = 0) and such that ![img](images/c03/inline1155.gif), where ![img](images/c03/inline1156.gif) is given by [(49)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0049) with ϕ = ϕ(0). These two extremals correspond to the curves of the function ϕ[θ] translated from each other by a given angle θ, which depends on the initial conditions. It is then straightforward to see that these two extremals intersect with the same length on the antipodal parallel π−ϕ(0). Using the fact that the evolution of the radial coordinate r depends only on ϕ and the symmetry ϕ→π−ϕ, one deduces that not only the projections, but also the extremal curves intersect with the same length that concludes the proof. ![img](images/squ.gif)

As a corollary of proposition 9, we have the following result about the cut locus in the case |γ[+]−Γ|<2.

Corollary 1 Two extremals with nonzero p [θ] intersecting with the same time cannot be optimal beyond the intersecting point.

Proof The proof is standard. Assuming optimality beyond the intersecting point, we can construct a broken minimizer that is an extremal with nonzero p[θ]. ![img](images/squ.gif)

The case |Γ−γ[+]|≥2

Extremal curves have a more complex behavior for |Γ−γ[+]|≥2\. We proceed as before by fixing p[θ], p[ρ], and H = h. The projections of the extremals on the sphere in the coordinates (ϕ, θ) are either aperiodic or periodic according to the values of p[θ], p[ρ], and h. Some general characteristics of the extremals can be determined and are described by the following results.

Aperiodic extremals are extremals such that ϕ is not periodic. They have an asymptotic fixed point (r[f], ϕ[f], θ[f]) and p[ϕ]→±∞, when t→+∞. The possible fixed points are described by the following proposition:

Proposition 10 The projection of the asymptotic fixed point of the extremal on the sphere in the coordinates (θ, ϕ) is located on one of the parallels ϕ[f] = α, ϕ[f] = π/2−α, ϕ[f] = π/2+α, and ϕ[f] = π−α, where α=arcsin[2/|Γ−γ[+]|]/2\. If Γ>γ[+] (respectively, Γ<γ[+]), then only the fixed points such that ϕ[f] = α or ϕ[f] = π−α (respectively, ϕ[f] = π/2−α or ϕ[f] = π/2+α) can be reached depending on the initial value ϕ(0).

Proof Proposition 10 can be shown by solving the following equation deduced from the system [(42)](#chapter03.html_c03-mdis-0042):

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0198.gif)

Then, we determine the sign of the expression 1−(γ[+]−Γ)²sin²(2ϕ)/4 as a function of ϕ and we deduce that the derivative dϕ/dθ has no zero in]α, π/2−α[∪]π/2+α, π−α[. For Γ−γ[+]>2, a direct inspection of dϕ/dθ also shows that dϕ/dθ<0 (respectively, dϕ/dθ>0) if ϕ![img](images/epsiv1.gif)]α, π/2−α[(respectively, ϕ![img](images/epsiv1.gif)]π/2+α, π−α[), which indicates the parallel that can be reached. The same analysis can be undertaken for Γ−γ[+]<2, which completes the proof. ![img](images/squ.gif)

Note that the aperiodic extremals have no conjugate point and are always optimal. Periodic extremals occur in a band near the equator. They have the same properties as the extremals for |Γ−γ[+]|<2\. In particular, two extremals of the same length intersect on the antipodal parallel.

The characteristics of the extremals are summarized in [Fig. 26](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0026). Two periodic trajectories intersecting with the same length on the antipodal parallel are displayed. Other extremals are aperiodic with fixed points located on the parallel ϕ[f]=arcsin[2/|Γ−γ[+]|]/2 or ϕ[f] = π−arcsin[2/|Γ−γ[+]|]/2.

[Figure 26.](#chapter03.html__back167) Extremal trajectories for Γ = 4.5 and γ[+] = 2\. Dashed lines represent the equator and the locus of the fixed points of the dynamics. The solid line corresponds to the antipodal parallel. Numerical values of the parameters are taken to be ϕ(0) = 2π/5, p[θ] = 8, r(0) = 1 and p[r](0) = 0.25\. The initial values of p[ϕ] are -50, -10, 0, 2.637, 3, 5, 10, and 50.

Numerical Determination of the Conjugate Locus

The goal of this section is to determine numerically the conjugate locus for the two-input case. We restrict the discussion to the case |Γ−γ[+]|<2\. Following the previous section, numerical computations are undertaken in spherical coordinates for fixed coordinates p[θ] and p[ρ]. As already mentioned, the case Γ=γ[+] can be associated to the Grushin model, the drift vector field F[0] being purely radial. In this model, the conjugate locus is known and described in Section II.C. This result is recalled in [Fig. 27](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0027), where we represent the projection of the conjugate and cut loci on the sphere of radius 1 in the coordinates (θ, ϕ). The radial dependence is trivial in this case and depends on the value of Γ=γ[+]. Here, the important point to note is the fact that the projection of the conjugate locus on the sphere is independent of p[r](0) for this model.

[Figure 27.](#chapter03.html__back168) Extremal trajectories for the Grushin model corresponding to Γ= γ[+] = 2\. Conjugate and cut loci are represented in dashed line.

![img](images/c03/nfg027.gif)

The idea is then to start from this model and to deform it by modifying the parameters γ[+] and Γ with the constraint |Γ−γ[+]|<2. A first comparison between the two models is given by [Figs. 27](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0027) and [28](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0028), where we observe that the global structure of the extremals is nearly the same. The same conclusion is obtained from the analysis of the radial coordinate whose evolution is not represented here. [Figure 29](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0029) displays the projection of the conjugate locus on the sphere in the coordinates (θ, ϕ) for a given value of p[r](0). We have added the locus of the Grushin model for comparison. Note that this locus is only slightly modified with respect to the one of the Grushin model. However, the projection on the sphere of the conjugate locus depends on p[r](0) for Γ≠γ[+].

[Figure 28.](#chapter03.html__back170) Extremal trajectories for Γ = 2.5 and γ[+] = 2\. The projection of the conjugate locus is represented in dashed lines. The horizontal dashed line is the line where two trajectories intersect with the same length. Numerical values for the parameters are taken to be ϕ(0)=π/4, p[r](0) = 0.5, and p[θ] = 2.

![img](images/c03/nfg028.gif)

[Figure 29.](#chapter03.html__back171) Projection of the conjugate locus is represented by a solid line for p[r] = 0.5. The conjugate locus of the Grushin model corresponding to γ[+]=Γ = 2 is represented in dashed lines. The horizontal dashed line indicates the position of the cut locus for the Grushin model. Dissipative parameters are taken to be Γ = 2.5, and γ[+] = 2\. p[θ] is equal to 2.

![img](images/c03/nfg029.gif)

Geometric Interpretation of the Integrable Case

A geometric analysis allows us a complete understanding of the two types of extremal behaviors. Indeed assuming γ[−] = 0, the restriction of the system to the two-sphere is

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0199.gif)

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0200.gif)

We observe that it defines a Zermelo navigation problem [57] on the sphere where the current is

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0201.gif)

and the metric is the Grushin metric on the two sphere

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0202.gif)

The drift can be compensated by a feedback if |γ[+]−Γ|<2\. The problem is symmetric with respect to the equator and we can restrict our analysis to the upper hemisphere. We observe that the amplitude of the current is maximum for ϕ = π/4, while it is minimum at the North pole and at the equator. More generally, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 11 For the system restricted to the sphere we have two cases:

1\. If |γ[+]−Γ|<2, it defines a Zermelo navigation problem on the whole sphere minus the equator for the Grushin metric on the sphere.

2\. If |γ[+]−Γ|>2, the current can be compensated in the north equator except in a band centered at ϕ = π/4, hence defining a Zermelo navigation problem near the equator and near the North pole.

To complete the analysis, it is sufficient to describe the following barrier phenomenon. Let ψ = π/2−ϕ and assume that ![img](images/c03/inline1600.gif). Starting at the equator for which ψ = 0, when ψ increases, we meet a barrier corresponding to the singularity of the vector field. For example, if γ[+]−Γ>0, then we have a barrier for 1=[−sin(2ϕ)(γ[+]−Γ)/2].

This result explains the two types of extremal behaviors observed in the numerical simulations.

*   If |γ[+]−Γ|<2, the extremal curves restricted to the two sphere are periodic curves, as in the Grushin case.
*   If |γ[+]−Γ|>2, we have two types of extremal curves: Near the equator, since the current can be compensated, we have periodic curves. But if the trajectory is entering in the band where the current cannot be compensated, the barrier phenomenon appears and we observe the asymptotic behaviors of the extremals.

2\. The Generic Case γ[−]≠0

In this section, we use mainly numerical simulations to describe extremal curves in the case γ[−]≠0. We concentrate on the description of the generic cases observed in the numerical simulations. We present numerical results about the behavior of extremal solutions and conjugate point analysis.

Extremal Trajectories

We begin by analyzing the structure of extremal trajectories. The description is based on a direct integration of the system. We observe two different asymptotic behaviors corresponding to stationary points of the dynamics that are described by the following results.

Proposition 12 In the case denoted (a), where |p[ϕ](t)|→+∞ when t→+∞, the asymptotic stationary points (ρ[f], ϕ[f], θ[f]) of the dynamics are given by ![img](images/c03/inline1360.gif) and ϕ[f]=arctan(1/Γ) if γ[−]>0 or ϕ[f] = π−arctan(1/Γ) if γ[−]<0.

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0203.gif)

where ε=±1 according to the sign of p[ϕ]. The quotient of the two equations leads to

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0204.gif)

which simplifies into

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0205.gif)

Using the fact that ϕ[f]![img](images/epsiv1.gif)]0, π[and γ[−]cosϕ[f]≥0, one arrives to ϕ[f]=arctan(1/Γ) if γ[−]>0 and ϕ[f] = π−arctan(1/Γ) if γ[−]<0\. From the equation

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0206.gif)

one finally obtains that

Proposition 13 In the case denoted (b), where lim[t→+∞]ϕ(t) = 0 or π, the asymptotic limit of the dynamics is characterized by ρ[f]=|γ[−]|/γ[+] and ϕ[f] = 0 if γ[−]>0 or ϕ[f] = π if γ[−]<0.

Proof Using the relation

![img](images/c03/c03-mdis-0208.gif)

one deduces that γ[−]cosϕ[f]≥0 and that ρ[f]=|γ[−]|/γ[+] if ϕ[f] = 0 or π.![img](images/squ.gif)

We have numerically checked that if |Γ−γ[+]|>2, then only case (a) is encountered, whereas if |Γ−γ[+]|<2, the extremals are described by case (b). One particularity of case (a) is the fact that the limit of the dynamics only depends on Γ and on the sign of γ[−] and not on ϕ(0) or γ[+]. The structure of the extremals is also simple in case (b) since the limit of ϕ is 0 or π independently of the values of Γ, γ[+] or γ[−]. The different behaviors of the extremals are illustrated in [Fig. 30](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0030) for the case |Γ−γ[+]|>2 and in [Fig. 31](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0031) for the case |Γ−γ[+]|<2. The corresponding optimal control fields ![img](images/c03/inline1158.gif) and ![img](images/c03/inline1159.gif) are represented in [Fig. 32](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0032) for case (a) and in [Fig. 33](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0033) for case (b). In [Fig. 32](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0032), note that the control ![img](images/c03/inline1160.gif) tends to 0, whereas ![img](images/c03/inline1161.gif) is close to −1 for t sufficiently large. This is due to the fact that |p[ϕ]|→+∞ when t→+∞ and can be easily checked from the definition of ![img](images/c03/inline1162.gif) and ![img](images/c03/inline1163.gif). We observe a similar behavior for case (b) in [Fig. 33](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0033). Here the control field ![img](images/c03/inline1164.gif) acquires a bang–bang structure that is related to the unbounded and oscillatory behavior of p[ϕ](t) (see [Fig. 33](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0033)).

Conjugate Points

The Cotcot code is used to evaluate the conjugate points [31]. This occurs only in case (b) and the numerical simulations show that the first conjugate points appear before an uniform number of oscillations of the ϕ variable. This phenomenon is represented on [Fig. 34](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0034). Cutting the trajectory at the first conjugate point avoids such a behavior. Note that due to the symmetry of revolution, the global optimality is lost for θ≤π.

[Figure 34.](#chapter03.html__back181) Plot of the extremals of [Fig. 32](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0032) up to the first conjugate point. The coordinates θ of the conjugate points are, respectively, 3.149, 3.116, 3.332, 3.386, and 3.535 for p[ϕ](0) equal to −10, −2.5, 0, 2.5, and 10\. The equations of the horizontal and vertical solid lines are, respectively, ϕ = π/2 and θ = π.

![img](images/c03/nfg034.gif)

3\. Singular Value Decomposition

To illustrate the determination of the position of the conjugate points, here we consider the singular value decomposition of the matrix formed by the two Jacobi fields. It is this matrix that is used to compute the conjugate points.

Let p(0) be the initial value of the adjoint vector. We consider the two Jacobi fields such that p(0)·δp(0) = 0\. We use the singular-value decomposition of the matrix of the two Jacobi fields to compute the two singular values. The rank of the matrix (δx[1], δx[2]) decreases by one when one of the singular values vanishes. To compute the singular values, we have used the function svd of the Matlab software. [Figures 35](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0035) – [38](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0038) show the evolution of the angle ϕ and the corresponding values of the singular values for different dissipative parameters. The singular value 1 is equal to 0 for the first conjugate point.

[Figure 35.](#chapter03.html__back182) Evolution of the angle ϕ as a function of time for Γ = 4.5, γ[+] = 2, and γ[−] = 0\. Initial values are taken to be ϕ(0) = 2π/5, p[r](0) = 0.25, p[θ] = 8, and p[ϕ](0) = 10. Evolution of the two singular values as a function of time. We observe no conjugate point.

![img](images/c03/nfg035.gif)

[Figure 36.](#chapter03.html__back197) Same as [Fig. 35](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0035) but for p[ϕ](0) = 2.637\. The vertical solid line indicates the position of the first conjugate point along the extremal trajectory.![img](images/c03/nfg036.gif)

![img](images/c03/nfg038.gif)

[Figure 37.](#chapter03.html__back198) Same as [Fig. 36](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0036) but for Γ = 2.5, γ[+] = 2, γ[−]=−0.5, ϕ(0)=π/4, p[r](0) = 0.1, p[θ] = 2 and p[ϕ](0)=−2.

4\. Continuation Method

The continuation method was both implemented in a simple numerical framework using MatLab routines (discretization of the parameter interval that can be normalized to [0, 1], and at each step the Newton routine was used to compute the root) and by using a smooth approach. In this second case, a unique shooting is necessary to initialize the continuation method.

For geometric reasons, the continuation method is splitted into two parts.

*   Continuation method for the reduced problem: The final purity is not controlled and the transversality condition p[ρ](t[f]) = 0 at the final time t[f] is added in the shooting equation.
*   Continuation method for the full problem. In this case, we have to check that the final purity belongs to the accessible set for every value of the dissipation parameters.

We present two numerical examples illustrating these two situations. The smooth and the discrete continuation approaches have been implemented in each case. By (p[ρf] = 0, ϕ[f], θ[f]) and (ρ[f], ϕ[f], θ[f]) we denote, respectively, the target states of the control in the first and second cases. In the first case, where the final purity is not fixed, we consider the homotopy method with respect to the parameter Γ and in the second case a continuation with respect to γ[−]. Knowing the starting point of the continuation method [p[ρ](0), p[ϕ](0), t[f]], we use the two continuation algorithms to determine new triplets (p[ρ](0), p[ϕ](0), t[f]) such that the system reaches the same target state when the dissipation parameters vary. The adjoint state p[θ] is kept fixed in the computation, while the value of the Hamiltonian, which remains positive, changes at each step. [Figures. 39](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0039) and [40](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0040) represent the different results we have obtained. In each case, the control duration is chosen small enough to be before the first conjugate point. Note the smooth evolution of the three parameters p[ϕ](0), p[ρ](0), and t[f] when the continuation parameters vary. The results of the two methods are very close to each other. The step size of the discrete approach is, respectively, taken to be 0.05 and 0.01 in the first and second cases. With this step size, which is not representative of our control problem (larger step size can be chosen), the Newton algorithm does not encounter any problem to converge. The shooting equation is solved with an accuracy better than 10^(−12) in the two continuation methods.

[Figure 39.](#chapter03.html__back184) Continuation with respect to Γ. The parameters of the initial point are Γ = 2.5, γ[+] = 2, γ[−]=−0.1, ϕ(0)=π/4, p[ρ](0)=−0.1564, p[θ] = 8 and p[ϕ](0)=−1. The initial time is t[f] = 0.4259\. The target state corresponds to ϕ[f] = 0.845096, θ[f] = 0.04015590, and p[ρf] = 0\. Three figures represent the evolution of the adjoint states p[ρ](0) and p[ϕ](0) and of the control duration t[f] as a function of Γ for the Newton-type continuation (dashed line) and the smooth-type one (solid line). The last figure displays the projection of the extremal trajectory solution of the continuation problem onto the Bloch sphere for Γ = 2.5 and γ[+] = 2.

![img](images/c03/nfg039.gif)

[Figure 40.](#chapter03.html__back185) Same as [Fig. 39](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0039), but for a continuation with respect to γ[−]. The parameters of the initial point are Γ = 2.5, γ[+] = 2, γ[−]=−0.1, ϕ(0)=π/4, p[ρ](0)=−10.0208, p[θ] = 8, and p[ϕ](0)=−1. The initial time is t[f] = 0.32\. The target state corresponds to ρ[f] = 0.47182, ϕ[f] = 0.77468, and θ[f] = 0.32774.

![img](images/c03/nfg040.gif)

In the second case, when we consider a final condition on the radial coordinate, some problems of accessibility have been encountered. As a preliminary study, we have therefore to check that the target state belongs to the accessibility set for every value of the dissipation parameters used in the continuation method. When this condition is satisfied, we have observed no problem of convergence for the continuation method. This condition is more difficult to fulfill for long control durations. [Figure 41](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0041) illustrates the constraint due to the accessibility set in the second case. For given values of dissipation parameters and of p[ρ], we have determined the values of p[ϕ](0) and t[f] allowing us to reach the point of coordinates (ϕ[f], θ[f]). We have then plotted the corresponding radial coordinate ρ[f] as a function of p[ρ]. Note that the diagram is symmetric with respect to the vertical axis p[ρ] = 0 and has roughly the same form up to vertical translations for different values of the dissipation parameters. [Figure 41](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0041) shows the values of the radial coordinate ρ[f] that can be reached when ϕ[f] and θ[f] are fixed. Using such a diagram for different dissipation parameters, one can check that the point (ρ[f], ϕ[f], θ[f]) belongs to the accessible set. [Figures 42](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0042) and [43](#chapter03.html_c03-fig-0043) represent the evolution of the continuation parameters as a function of the number of steps. The step size of the Newton algorithm has been chosen so that the number of steps of the two approaches are roughly the same. Note the nontrivial behavior of the continuation parameter in the second case.

[Figure 41.](#chapter03.html__back186) Plot of ρ[f] as a function of p[ρ](0) for Γ = 2.5, γ[+] = 2, and γ[−] = 0.1\. Other parameters are taken to be ϕ(0)=π/4, p[θ] = 8, ϕ[f] = 0.77468, and θ[f] = 0.32774\. The equation of the horizontal line is ρ[f] = 0.47182, which corresponds to the target state.

![img](images/c03/nfg041.gif)

[Figure 42.](#chapter03.html__back188) Evolution of the continuation parameter Γ as a function of the number of steps for the discrete method (solid line) and the smooth one (dashed line).

V. Conclusion

We hope that these different examples of application of techniques of geometric optimal control theory to simple quantum systems will motivate a systematic investigation of the geometric aspects in quantum control. We have investigated the optimal control of a conservative three-level quantum system and the time-optimal control of two-level dissipative quantum systems. In this context, an open question is the generalization of the present examples to more complex quantum systems having, for example, four or five levels or corresponding to two or three spins in interaction [17]. Due to the complexity of such systems, the determination of the optimal control law will require a more extensive numerical analysis than the one presented in this chapter. The same tools as the continuation method or the second-order optimality conditions can be used in this case.

Chapter 4

Density Matrix Equation for a Bathed Small System and its Application to Molecular Magnets

D. A.Garanin

Department of Physics and Astronomy Lehman College, City University of New York, Bronx, New York 10468-1589

I. General Theory

Density matrix is used to describe properties of a system that is a part of a larger system with which it interacts. Whereas isolated systems (e.g., the above mentioned larger system) can be described by a Schrödinger equation, systems that interact with their environments cannot. Starting from the Schrödinger equation for the isolated whole system, small system + environment (or bath), and eliminating the environmental variables, one can, in principle, construct an object that can be used to calculate observables of the small system, in a short way. This object is the density matrix of the small system. Of course, integrating or taking matrix elements in two steps, at first over the environment and then over the small system, is not a big simplification. This approach becomes really useful if one obtains a closed equation of motion for the density matrix of the small system, the density matrix equation (DME). This is possible if the interaction between the small system and its environment is small and can be considered as a perturbation, and the small system does not strongly perturb the state of the environment. The derivation of the DME for a bathed small system will be presented in Section II. Here the necessary components of the formalism will be introduced.

[(11)](#chapter04.html__back8) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0011.gif)

where |ψ[m]![img](images/rquo.gif) is a complete orthogonal set of states. The density matrix consists of matrix elements of the density operator:

[(12)](#chapter04.html__back18) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0012.gif)

so that both DM and DO contain the same information and are equivalent. For a system in a pure state with the wave function |ψ![img](images/rquo.gif), the density operator defined by [Eq. [(19)](#chapter04.html__back15) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0019.gif)

where Z is the partition function. Because of applications in quantum statistics, the density operator is also called the statistical operator.

In obtaining [Eq. (19)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0019), it is tacitly assumed that the whole system (small system + bath) is surrounded by the so-called superbath. A very small coupling to the superbath (that is never considered explicitly) ensures that the whole system tends to thermal equilibrium with the temperature T of the superbath. On the other hand, this coupling is so small that it does not influence the dynamics of the whole system. This dictates the dual approach: Dynamically the whole system is considered as a pure (isolated) system, but then it is assumed that the bath is at thermal equilibrium.

We will concentrate on the common case in which the coupling ![img](images/c04/inline041.gif) in [Eq. (18)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0018) is a small perturbation. Then ![img](images/c04/inline041.gif) can be neglected in [Eq. (19)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0019) and the equilibrium DO factorizes:

(20) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0020.gif)

where, for example,

(21) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0021.gif)

where Z is the partition function of the small system. Defining the density matrix ![img](images/c04/inline027.gif) with respect to the basis of eigenstates of ![img](images/c04/inline048.gif) in [Eq. (12)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0012), one arrives at [Eq. (10)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0010). The eigenstate basis is the most convenient for calculating thermal averages of physical quantities. However, as pointed out above, this could be done with the help of any other basis.

The role of the small interaction ![img](images/c04/inline045.gif) neglected in [Eq. (19)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0019) is in providing the source of relaxation of the small system toward the equilibrium from any initial state. To describe the dynamics of this process, we will need the interaction representation considered in Section I.C.

C. Temporal Evolution and Interaction Representation

Temporal evolution of the DM or DO of an isolated system, such as the small system + bath, obeys the equation that follows from the Schrödinger equation. If, for example, the whole system is in a pure state |Ψ![img](images/rquo.gif), its density operator is given by

(22) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0022.gif)

cf. [Eq. (13)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0013). Then with the help of the Schrödinger equation and its conjugate

[(23)](#chapter04.html__back22) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0023.gif)

one obtains the quantum Liouville equation

[(24)](#chapter04.html__back23) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0024.gif)

Note that this equation is not an equation of motion for an operator in the Heisenberg representation that has another sign. The DO consists of states that have their own time dependence, unlike Heisenberg operators, whose time dependence is borrowed from the states.

More generally, the whole system (still isolated) can be considered as a mixture of different states |Ψ![img](images/rquo.gif),

(25) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0025.gif)

Using [Eq. (23)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0023), one again obtains [Eq. (24)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0024). The above form of ![img](images/c04/inline095.gif) is needed to justify the equilibrium form of the DO in [Eq. (19)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0019). The mixture of states can be considered as arising from the (vanishingly small) coupling of the bath to the so-called superbath that sets the temperature T of the bath.

II. The Density Matrix Equation

A. From the Full-to-Reduced DOE

In this section, the equation of motion for the reduced density operator of a small system s weakly interacting with a bath b will be obtained. We will be following the method descibed by Karl Blum [1] that is most practical, although not rigorous. Rigorous methods using the projection operator technique [2, 3] lead to the same result with much greater efforts.

[Equation (30)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0030) for the DO of the whole system can be integrated over the time resulting in the integral equation

(32) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0032.gif)

Inserting this formal solution back into [Eq. (30)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0030) one obtains the integrodifferential equation

[(33)](#chapter04.html__back36) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0033.gif)

which is still exact. Although this equation seems to be more complicated than the initial [equation (30)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0030), it is convenient for perturbative treatment of the interaction ![img](images/c04/inline044.gif) The density operator of the whole system can be searched for in the factorized form, additionally using the fact that the bath is at thermal equilibrium that cannot be noticeably distorted by a weak interaction with the small subsystem:

[(34)](#chapter04.html__back37) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0034.gif)

Here, Z[b] is the partition function of the bath. The role of the coupling ![img](images/c04/inline044.gif) is in providing the relaxation of ![img](images/c04/inline072.gif) toward equilibrium, whereas the small effect of nonfactorization of the whole density operator can be neglected. Indeed, the equilibrium density matrix of the whole system is given by [Eq. (19)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0019) that does not exactly factorize because of ![img](images/c04/inline044.gif) in the Hamiltonian ![img](images/c04/inline074.gif) However, this nonfactorization is a small effect, while the right part of [Eq. (33)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0033) already contains ![img](images/c04/inline063.gif) squared. On the other hand, ![img](images/c04/inline076.gif) undergoes essential changes because of relaxation that are captured within the present approach.

Now, using [Eq. (34)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0034) one can transform [Eq. (33)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0033) into the density operator equation for the small system by making a trace over the bath variables according to [Eq. (16)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0016):

For the couplings ![img](images/c04/inline075.gif) we consider here, the first linear-![img](images/c04/inline075.gif) term disappears.

The equation above is an integrodifferential equation with integration over preceding times, t′≤t, in the right-hand side (rhs). Such equations are called equations with memory and they are difficult to solve directly. In our case, however, the problem simplifies. The time evolution of ![img](images/c04/inline080.gif) in [Eq. (35)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0035) is slow since it is governed by the weak interaction between the system and the bath. On the other hand, t′ dependences of the other terms in the integrand (the kernel) are governed by ![img](images/c04/inline082.gif) and thus they are fast at the scale of ![img](images/c04/inline080.gif) The analysis shows that the kernel in [Eq. (35)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0035) is localized in the region ![img](images/c04/inline084.gif) where ω[max] is the maximal frequency of the bath excitations. Thus in the integral over t′ one can make the short-memory approximation ![img](images/c04/inline087.gif) after which the time integral can be calculated analytically.

D. Secular Approximation and Fermi Golden Rule

One can transform [Eq. (52)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0052) back into the interaction representation using [Eqs. (29)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0029) and [(12)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0012). In the diagonal basis, the relation between ρ[αβ](t) and ρ[αβ](t)[I] has the simple form

[(55)](#chapter04.html__back64) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0055.gif)

Computing the time derivative of ρ[αβ](t)[I] and using [Eq. (52)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0052), one arrives at the equation

[(56)](#chapter04.html__back64) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0056.gif)

where the conservative term disappeared and the relaxation term has an explicit time dependence. While the change of the density matrix due to the relaxation is slow, the oscillation in the terms with ![img](images/c04/inline070.gif) are generally fast. These fast oscillating terms average out and make a negligible contribution into the dynamics of the small system. In general, all transition frequencies are nondegenerate, so that one can drop all terms with α≠α′ and β≠β′, if α≠β. In the equations for the diagonal terms ρ[αα](t)[I] one can keep only diagonal terms with α′=β′. This is the secular approximation that greatly simplifies the DME. In the secular approximation, one has

[(57)](#chapter04.html__back60) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0057.gif)

E. Analysis of the Nonsecular DME

We have seen above that within the secular approximation nondiagonal DM elements are decoupled from diagonal ones and oscillate with decay independently of each other. In the full DME of [Eq. (52)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0052), nondiagonal DM elements are coupled to the diagonal elements. Nevertheless, they approach zero at equilibrium, in spite of diagonal elements being nonzero. This points to an interesting feature of the full DME that has to be worked out in more detail. Separating diagonal and nondiagonal elements in the relaxation term of [Eq. (52)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0052), one obtains

(70) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0070.gif)

With the use of energy conservation, this can be rewritten as:

[(71)](#chapter04.html__back115) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0071.gif)

One can see that as the diagonal DM elements approach their equilibrium values defined by [Eq. (68)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0068), they cease to drive nondiagonal elements. Thus the equilibrium solution of the full DME is [Eq. (54)](#chapter04.html_c04-mdis-0054).

F. Semisecular Approximation

In a system with tunnel-split energy levels, such as molecular magnets, there are very small transition frequencies ω[αβ] and correspondingly very slow nondiagonal matrix elements ρ[αβ] whose dynamics is coupled to that of the diagonal elements. While the secular approximation neglects the interaction between diagonal and slow nondiagonal DM elements that can lead to nonphysical results for relaxation rates, the full nonsecular formalism involves a big N²×N² matrix. In important particular cases, such as thermal activation over a barrier or tunneling, the eigenvalues of the DM span a broad range from very fast to very slow, the latter being of primary importance in relaxation. Because of this, one has to do numerical calculations with increased precision, which makes them very slow. This difficulty can be overcome with the help of the semisecular approximation that considers coupled equations for diagonal and slow nondiagonal DM elements plus decoupled equations for the fast DM elements. The easiest way to implement it is to include the diagonal and subdiagonal terms ρ[αα] and ρ[α,α±1] into the slow group, because in most situations there are only two levels that come close to each other, making ρ[α,α+1] or ρ[α,α−1] slow. Implementation of the semisecular DME in the case of time-independent ![img](images/c04/inline153.gif) will be covered in Section III.A.3.

III. Time-Dependent Problems

In this section, we consider the DME and its solution in two important cases: (1) free evolution for time-independent ![img](images/c04/inline082.gif); (2) periodic or nonperiodic slow perturbation. In both of these cases the solution can be obtained by matrix algebra. In contrast, problems with a large temporal change of ![img](images/c04/inline156.gif) cannot be solved by matrix algebra. The secular, nonsecular, and semisecular versions of the DME yield the same results except for the case of anomalously close energy levels (e.g., tunnel split levels). In the latter case, the semisecular DME is preferred, while in general the fastest and easiest secular DME is the best choice.

Results of this section are general. Numerical examples will be given later for molecular magnets after their spin–lattice coupling is explained.

B. Linear Response

In this section, we consider a small harmonic perturbation

(110) ![equation](images/c04/c04-mdis-0110.gif)

acting on the small system. The frequency ω of the perturbation can be close or not to the resonance with any transition ω[αβ]. In the former case, the response to the perturbation is of a resonance character and is effectuated by the presence of ![img](images/c04/inline219.gif) in the conservative term of the DME. If ω<ω[αβ] for all α, β, the response of the small system is due to the relaxation and depends on the relation between ω and the relaxation rate Γ. To take account of this effect, one has to include ![img](images/c04/inline225.gif) into the relaxation terms of the DME. Temporal change of ![img](images/c04/inline228.gif) changes the instantaneous equilibrium to which the system relaxes that gives rise to a dissipative dynamics. Similar to the case of free evolution, Section III.A, the formalism can be developed within the secular, semisecular, and nonsecular approximations. The secular approximation will be used below for simplicity. It will be shown how the method can be generalized for the nonsecular DME.
